Development of Implosions of Solutions to the Three-Dimensional Degenerate Compressible Navier-Stokes Equations
Gui-Qiang G. Chen, Lihui Liu, Shengguo Zhu

Abstract
. A fundamental open problem in the theory of the multidimensional compressible Navier–Stokes equations is whether smooth solutions can develop singularities—such as cavitation or implosion—in finite time. In the case of constant viscosity coefficients, recent remarkable results show that there exist some smooth initial data for which the corresponding smooth solutions of the barotropic flow undergo finite-time implosion at the origin in three spatial dimensions, with the density ρ \rho blowing up to infinity (see [ 38 , 2 , 43 , 5 ] ). On the other hand, when the viscosity coefficients depend linearly on ρ \rho (as in the shallow water equations), it has also been established recently that, for general large spherically symmetric initial data (even allowing vacuum states), the solutions of the barotropic flow remain globally regular in two and three spatial dimensions (see [ 8 , 9 ] ). These observations indicate that the behavior of the multi-dimensional solutions depends sensitively on the structures of the viscosity coefficients, thereby complicating the analysis of singularity formation. In this paper, we analyze the case of nonlinear density-dependent viscosity coefficients ( i.e. , ρ δ \rho^{\delta} with an exponent δ > 0 \delta>0 ). We identify a value δ ∗ ​ ( γ ) < 1 2 \delta^{*}(\gamma)<\frac{1}{2} , depending on the adiabatic exponent γ \gamma , such that, for every 0 < δ < δ ∗ ​ ( γ ) 0<\delta<\delta^{*}(\gamma) , there exists a class of smooth initial data for which the corresponding smooth solutions of the barotropic flow implode (with infinite density) in finite time at the origin in three spatial dimensions. Moreover, we assume that the initial density is strictly positive, in order to rule out the possibility that the corresponding implosion is an artifact of the vacuum. The key point is to prove that, in this regime, the degenerate viscous terms are not strong enough to suppress the convective mechanism driving the implosion. However, verifying this issue is challenging due to fundamental difficulties arising from the degenerate structure. Specifically, for flows with constant viscosity coefficients (as in [ 38 , 2 , 43 , 5 ] ), the dissipative effect in the momentum equation remains spatially uniform. In contrast, in our setting, the dissipative effect increases significantly in regions of large density. Therefore, one would expect the viscous terms to suppress any tendency toward implosion (just as in [ 8 , 9 ] ). To overcome this obstacle, we first provide a pointwise estimate of ρ \rho by a region segmentation method, and then establish the spatial decay estimates for the gradient of the velocity via carefully designed weighted estimates for high-order derivatives and interpolation inequalities. The corresponding decay rate is fast enough to absorb the singularity of ρ \rho and then leads to a uniform-in-time control of the viscous terms.



.

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 35A01, 35B40; Secondary: 35B65, 35A09

1.
Introduction

We are concerned with the formation of singularities in compressible viscous flows
and prove that there exists a class of smooth initial data for which the corresponding
smooth solutions of the Cauchy problem for the three-dimensional (3-D) compressible
Navier–Stokes equations (
CNS
) undergo finite-time implosion
within a physical parameter regime.
More specifically, we consider the motion of a barotropic, compressible, viscous Newtonian polytropic
fluid in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, governed by the following
CNS
:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\rho u)=0,\\[6.0pt]
\partial_{t}(\rho u)+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\rho u\otimes u)+\nabla_{x}P=\mathrm{div}_{x}\mathbb{S},\end{cases}

(1.1)

and the initial and far-field conditions:

\begin{split}&(\rho,u)(0,x)=(\rho_{0},u_{0})(x)\qquad\,\,\text{for $x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}$},\\[3.0pt]
\displaystyle&(\rho,u)(t,x)\to(\bar{\rho},0)\qquad\ \ \ \ \quad\,\text{as $|x|\to\infty$ for $t\geq 0$}.\end{split}

(1.2)

Here,
t\geq 0
denotes the time,

x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the Eulerian spatial coordinates,

\rho\geq 0
is the fluid density,
\bar{\rho}>0
is a constant,
u=(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})^{\top}

\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
is
the fluid velocity, and
P
denotes the fluid pressure. For polytropic gases, the constitutive relation is given by

P=A\rho^{\gamma},

(1.3)

where
A>0
is the entropy constant and
\gamma>1
is the adiabatic exponent.
Without loss of generality, by scaling, we may take
A=\frac{1}{\gamma}
.
The viscous stress tensor
\mathbb{S}
takes the form:

\mathbb{S}=2\mu(\rho)D_{x}(u)+\lambda(\rho)\,\mathrm{div}_{x}u\,\mathbb{I}_{3},

(1.4)

where
D_{x}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\big(\nabla_{x}u+(\nabla_{x}u)^{\top}\big)
is the deformation tensor,
\mathbb{I}_{3}
is the
3\times 3
identity matrix,

\mu(\rho)=a_{1}\rho^{\delta},\quad\lambda(\rho)=a_{2}\rho^{\delta}\qquad\,\,\mbox{for some constant $\delta\geq 0$},

(1.5)

\mu(\rho)
is the shear viscosity coefficient,

\lambda(\rho)+\frac{2}{3}\mu(\rho)
is the bulk viscosity coefficient,
and
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are both constants satisfying

a_{1}>0,\qquad 2a_{1}+3a_{2}\geq 0.

(1.6)

For rarefied gases, the full
CNS
can be formally derived from the Boltzmann equation
via the Chapman–Enskog expansion (see Chapman–Cowling
[
6
]
and Li–Qin
[
29
]
).
Under proper physical assumptions, the viscosity coefficients
(\mu,\lambda)
and the thermal conductivity coefficient
\kappa
are all functions
of the absolute temperature
\vartheta
. In fact,
for the cutoff inverse power force models, if the intermolecular potential varies as
\ell^{-\varkappa}
,
where
\ell
is the intermolecular distance and
\varkappa
is a positive constant, then

\mu(\vartheta)=b_{1}\vartheta^{\frac{1}{2}+b},\quad\lambda(\vartheta)=b_{2}\vartheta^{\frac{1}{2}+b},\quad\kappa(\vartheta)=b_{3}\vartheta^{\frac{1}{2}+b}\qquad\,\,\text{with}\,\,\,b=\frac{2}{\varkappa}\in[0,\infty),

(1.7)

for some constants
b_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
.
In particular, for the ionized gas,

\varkappa=1
;
for Maxwellian molecules,

\varkappa=4
;
while
\varkappa=\infty
for rigid elastic spherical molecules (see §10 of
[
6
]
).
For barotropic and polytropic fluids, such a dependence is inherited through the laws of Boyle and Gay-Lussac:

P=\hat{A}\rho\vartheta=A\rho^{\gamma}\qquad\text{for a constant }\hat{A}>0,

i.e.
,
\vartheta=A\hat{A}^{-1}\rho^{\gamma-1}
,
and the viscosity coefficients become functions of
\rho
taking form
(
1.5
)
; see Liu–Xin–Yang
[
33
]
.
Note that there exist other physical models satisfying the density-dependent viscosity assumption (
1.5
), such as the Korteweg system, the shallow water equations, the lake equations, the quantum Navier-Stokes system, among others; see
[
15
,
20
,
23
,
25
,
35
,
19
]
.

The local well-posedness of strong solutions of the Cauchy problem for
CNS
has been thoroughly studied.
When
\inf_{x}{\rho_{0}(x)}>0
, the local well-posedness of smooth solutions in the inhomogeneous Hilbert spaces
H^{s}
(
s>\frac{n}{2}+1
) follows from the standard symmetric hyperbolic-parabolic structure that
satisfies the well-known Kawashima’s condition (
cf.
Kawashima
[
24
]
and Nash
[
39
]
).
However, such approaches fail in the presence of vacuum due to the possible degeneracies
from the time evolution and spatial dissipation as follows:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\rho(\partial_{t}u+u\cdot\nabla_{x}u)}_{Degenerate\ time\ evolution}+\nabla_{x}P=\underbrace{\text{div}(2\mu(\rho)D_{x}(u)+\lambda(\rho)\,\mathrm{div}_{x}u\,\mathbb{I}_{n})}_{Degenerate\ spatial\ dissipation}.

(1.8)

Generally, a vacuum appears in the far-field under some physical requirements such as a finite total mass in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
For the multidimensional (M-D)
CNS
with constant viscosity coefficients (
\delta=0
in (
1.5
)), by introducing some compatibility conditions, the local well-posedness of smooth solutions
with vacuum in the homogeneous Sobolev spaces has been established successfully;
see Salvi–Straškraba
[
42
]
and Cho–Kim
[
11
]
, among others.
On the other hand, for M-D
CNS
with degenerate viscosity coefficients (
\delta>0
in (
1.5
)),
by introducing some enlarged reformulations to deal with the degenerate spatial dissipation,
the local well-posedness of smooth solutions with vacuum in the inhomogeneous Sobolev spaces
has been established by Zhu
[
53
]
, Li–Pan–Zhu
[
30
,
31
]
, Xin–Zhu
[
51
]
, and the references cited therein.

Another fundamental problem in the theory of fluid dynamics is the question of global regularity in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(
n\geq 2
).
Under proper smallness assumptions on the initial data,
some important progress has been made on the global well-posedness of smooth solutions
to the M-D
CNS
;
see Matsumura–Nishida
[
36
]
, Danchin
[
14
]
, and Huang–Li–Xin
[
21
]
for flows with constant viscosity coefficients, and Sundbye
[
46
]
, Wang–Xu
[
48
]
, and Xin–Zhu
[
50
]
for flows with degenerate viscosity coefficients.
On the other hand, for the general smooth initial data, very few results are available concerning
the global well-posedness of smooth solutions to the M-D
CNS
.
Recently, when the viscosity coefficients depend linearly on the mass
density
\rho
(as in the shallow water equations,
i.e.
, the viscous Saint-Venant system
with
\delta=1
and
a_{2}=0
in (
1.5
)),
Chen–Zhang–Zhu
[
8
]
have established global well-posedness of spherically symmetric smooth solutions of the Cauchy problem in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(
n=2
or
3
) for the following two classes of initial density profiles:

(i)

Strictly positive density in the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho_{0}(x)>0;

(ii)

Positive density decaying at infinity:

\rho_{0}(x)>0\quad\text{for $x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$},\qquad\quad\rho_{0}(x)\rightarrow 0\quad\text{as $|x|\rightarrow\infty$}.

Furthermore, for the degenerate
CNS
(
1.1
) with
\delta=1
in (
1.5
),
even when the initial density vanishes in some open set, under the setting of the vacuum free boundary problem, Chen–Zhang–Zhu have proved in the recent work
[
9
]
that
the solutions with large data of spherical symmetry still remain globally regular and unique
in two and three spatial dimensions.
It is worth emphasizing that, for the global-in-time well-posedness established
in
[
9
]
, the physical vacuum boundary condition is allowed for the class of initial
data considered, and the corresponding solutions remain smooth all the way up
to and including the moving vacuum boundary.
For the general M-D problem with large initial data,
although many important results on the global existence of weak solutions
of the Cauchy problem have been obtained (see
[
1
,
17
,
18
,
22
,
28
,
32
,
47
]
),
the issue of uniqueness remains widely open due to the limited regularity of these weak solutions.

We remark
that there are several results concerning the singularity formation for the M-D
CNS
.
For the flow with constant viscosity coefficients, Xin
[
49
]
showed that any smooth solutions in the inhomogeneous Sobolev spaces to the non-isentropic
CNS
with zero thermal conductivity cannot exist globally in time, since it may blow up in finite time, provided that
the density is compactly supported, which holds regardless of the size of the initial data.
Subsequently, Rozanova
[
40
]
showed that the smooth solutions of the Cauchy problem for

CNS
in three or higher spatial dimensions, with conserved total mass, finite total energy, and finite moment of inertia, lose their initial smoothness in finite time,
even when the initial density is not compactly supported.
On the other hand, it remains unclear whether the class of solutions considered
in
[
40
,
49
]
admits the local-in-time existence in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(
n\geq 2
).
For the barotropic
CNS
with degenerate viscosity coefficients (
\delta>1
in (
1.5
)), it was shown in
[
31
,
50
]
that,
for certain classes of 3-D initial data with vacuum in some open set, one can construct corresponding local smooth solutions in the inhomogeneous Sobolev spaces that
break down in finite time, regardless of the size of the initial data.

These observations indicate that, in the presence of vacuum,
under the setting of the Cauchy problem,
viscous effects are insufficient to prevent the singularity formation of
classical solutions to
CNS
for the cases
\delta=0
and
\delta>1
in (
1.5
). However, the results obtained in
[
40
,
49
,
31
,
50
]

show that the lifespan of the corresponding classical solutions is finite,
typically via contradiction arguments,
and thus do not provide a detailed description of the underlying blow-up mechanism.
If the viscous effects are formally neglected in compressible flow,
the motion of the corresponding barotropic inviscid flow is governed by
the compressible Euler equations (system (
1.1
) with
a_{1}=a_{2}=0
).
For this inviscid system, the mechanism of singularity formation has been studied extensively and deeply.
In particular, significant progress has been made over the past decades
on the shock formation;
see, for example, the works of Lax
[
26
]
, Lebaud
[
27
]
,
Sideris
[
45
]
,
Chen–Dong
[
10
]
, Yin
[
52
]
,
Christodoulou
[
12
]
, Luk–Speck
[
34
]
, Christodoulou–Miao
[
13
]
,
Chen–Chen–Zhu
[
7
]
, Buckmaster–Shkoller–Vicol
[
3
,
4
]
,
Shkoller–Vicol
[
44
]
, and the references cited therein.

In addition to the shock formation, another mechanism of singularity formation in
compressible flows, known as implosion, has been observed.
In this scenario, either the density or the velocity becomes unbounded at the blow-up time.
Recently, Merle–Raphaël–Rodnianski–Szeftel
[
37
]
constructed
C^{\infty}
smooth,
spherically symmetric imploding solutions to the barotropic compressible Euler equations
for
\gamma>1
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
except for at most countably many values of
\gamma
,
based on a self-similar reformulation to the equations.
Specifically, denoting
\alpha=\frac{\gamma-1}{2}
and introducing the variable

c=\alpha^{-1}\rho^{\alpha},

the Euler equations (system (
1.1
) with
a_{1}=a_{2}=0
) can be rewritten as

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}c=-u\cdot\nabla_{x}c-\alpha c\,\mathrm{div}_{x}u,\\[6.0pt]
\partial_{t}u=-u\cdot\nabla_{x}u-\alpha c\nabla_{x}c.\end{cases}

(1.9)

Then, for fixed constants
T>0
and
\Lambda>1
, one can introduce the vector function
(Q,U)

by

\displaystyle c(t,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}-1}}{\Lambda}Q(-\frac{\log(T-t)}{\Lambda},\frac{x}{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}}),

(1.10)

\displaystyle u(t,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}-1}}{\Lambda}U(-\frac{\log(T-t)}{\Lambda},\frac{x}{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}}),

and the new self-similar time and space coordinates
(\tau,y)
:

\tau=-\frac{\log(T-t)}{\Lambda},\quad y=\frac{x}{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}}=e^{\tau}x,

(1.11)

with the initial time
\tau_{0}=-\frac{\log T}{\Lambda}>0
,
where
y=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\tau\in[\tau_{0},\infty)
.
In the self-similar coordinates
(\tau,y)
, the compressible Euler equations become

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}Q=-(\Lambda-1)Q-(y+U)\cdot\nabla_{y}Q-\alpha Q\,\mathrm{div}_{y}U,\\[6.0pt]
\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}U=-(\Lambda-1)U-(y+U)\cdot\nabla_{y}U-\alpha Q\,\nabla_{y}Q.\end{cases}

(1.12)

In
[
37
]
, for
\gamma\in(1,\,\infty)
except for at most countably many points, there exists a discrete sequence
\{\Lambda_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
satisfying

1<\Lambda_{n}<\Lambda^{*}(\gamma),\qquad\,\,\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Lambda_{n}=\Lambda^{*}(\gamma),

(1.13)

with

\Lambda^{*}(\gamma)=\begin{cases}1+\frac{2}{(1+\sqrt{\frac{2}{\gamma-1}})^{2}}&\,\,\text{for}\,\,1<\gamma<\frac{5}{3},\\[6.0pt]
\frac{3\gamma-1}{2+\sqrt{3}(\gamma-1)}&\,\,\text{for}\,\,\gamma\geq\frac{5}{3},\end{cases}

(1.14)

such that, for every
\Lambda_{n}
, nontrivial
C^{\infty}
smooth spherically symmetric profiles
(\overline{Q}_{n},\overline{U}_{n})
were constructed
by solving

\begin{cases}(\Lambda_{n}-1)\overline{Q}_{n}+(y+\overline{U}_{n})\cdot\nabla_{y}\overline{Q}_{n}+\alpha\overline{Q}_{n}\,\mathrm{div}_{y}\overline{U}_{n}=0,\\[6.0pt]
(\Lambda_{n}-1)\overline{U}_{n}+(y+\overline{U}_{n})\cdot\nabla_{y}\overline{U}_{n}+\alpha\overline{Q}_{n}\,\nabla_{y}\overline{Q}_{n}=0.\end{cases}

(1.15)

These profiles are regular at the origin and decay at spatial infinity. In
particular,

\overline{Q}_{n}(0)=Q_{*}>0,\qquad\overline{U}_{n}(0)=0,\ \qquad(\overline{Q}_{n},\overline{U}_{n})(y)\to(0,0)\quad\text{as $|y|\to\infty$}.

The existence of such self-similar profiles implies that the corresponding
C^{\infty}
solutions to the compressible Euler equations (
1.9
)
undergo finite-time implosion. For the 3-D barotropic
CNS
with constant viscosity coefficients
and spherical symmetry, based on the existence of the
C^{\infty}
smooth self-similar solutions
to the compressible Euler equations in
[
37
]
,
Merle–Raphaël–Rodnianski–Szeftel
[
38
]
proved that, for
\gamma\in\big(1,\,1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\big)
except for at most countably many points, there exists a class of smooth finite-energy initial data with far-field vacuum such that
the corresponding solutions undergo finite-time implosion, with the density blowing up to infinity.

It is worth noting that the case
\gamma=\frac{5}{3}
, corresponding to a monatomic gas,
was excluded in
[
37
,
38
]

due to a triple-point degeneracy in the underlying analysis.
This gap was later filled by Shao–Wang–Wei–Zhang
[
43
]
by introducing a novel renormalization
for the autonomous system (
B.3
) in Appendix
B
. In particular, it was proved in
[
43
]
that, for
\gamma=\frac{5}{3}
,
the sequence
\{\Lambda_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
described in (
1.13
)–(
1.15
)
also exists, which can be used to show the finite-time blow-up of solutions of the 3-D barotropic
CNS
.
Moreover, for 3-D barotropic flow, Buckmaster–Cao-Labora–Gómez-Serrano in
[
2
]

constructed smooth self-similar imploding solutions to the compressible Euler equations for all
\gamma>1
,
and subsequently proved the finite-time blow-up of solutions of
CNS
with strictly positive initial density
and spherical symmetry when
\gamma=\frac{7}{5}
(corresponding to a diatomic gas).
On the other hand, for the 3-D barotropic
CNS
without any symmetry assumption,
Cao-Labora–Gómez-Serrano–Shi–Staffilani
[
5
]
constructed some smooth solutions
that remain strictly away from vacuum and nevertheless develop an imploding finite-time
singularity in
\mathbb{T}^{3}
or
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
These developments indicate that, although viscosity generally has a regularizing effect,
the self-similar blow-up mechanisms identified in the inviscid setting
may still persist or influence the dynamics in weakly viscous regimes.

These results naturally raise the question of whether similar finite-time singularities persist
in the physically important case that the viscosity coefficients depend on the density
in a power law (
i.e.
, the form:
\rho^{\delta}
with an exponent
\delta>0
).
Indeed, when vacuum appears in some open set, finite-time blow-up phenomena have been observed
for the case
\delta>1
in (
1.5
) (see
[
31
,
50
]
).
However, from a physical standpoint, the concept of fluid velocity itself loses meaning in the region where the density vanishes.
In the derivation of hydrodynamic equations from first principles,
a fundamental underlying assumption is that
the fluid is non-dilute and can be described as a continuum. This continuum hypothesis fails in vacuum regions, where the hydrodynamic
description no longer applies to the evolution of thermodynamic states.
Consequently, the dynamics within vacuum regions cannot be meaningfully analyzed using classical hydrodynamic models.
Based on the above considerations, in the setting of the Cauchy problem, it is natural to expect the
viscous system may fail to be globally well-posed when vacuum is present in an open set at the initial time.
From this viewpoint, the finite-time blow-up results obtained for the Cauchy problem
in
[
31
,
50
]
for the case
\delta>1
in (
1.5
)
are perhaps not unexpected.
What is of genuine interest, however, is the regime in which the initial data remain strictly away from the vacuum in the domain under consideration.
In contrast to the case
\delta>1
,
for the degenerate
CNS
(
1.1
) with
\delta=1
in (
1.5
)
in two and three spatial dimensions, as mentioned above, no matter for the Cauchy problems that
are strictly away from the vacuum or allow the far-field vacuum in
[
8
]
,
or the vacuum free boundary problem that contains the physical vacuum in
[
9
]
,
the corresponding smooth solutions with large initial data of spherical symmetry remain
globally regular and unique.
These results demonstrate that the qualitative behavior of solutions
to the M-D degenerate
CNS
depends sensitively on the viscosity exponent
\delta
,
thereby substantially complicating the analysis of singularity formation
in the density-dependent viscosity setting.

In this paper, we study the Cauchy problem for the M-D
CNS
with the viscosity coefficients determined by (
1.5
) involving
\rho^{\delta}
with
\delta>0
.
A fundamental difficulty in this problem arises from the fact that the dissipative effects become
increasingly strong in the regions of large density. From a formal perspective,
one would therefore expect the dissipative terms to dominate the dynamics
and suppress any tendency toward implosion.

Beyond this physical intuition, the rigorous analysis presents substantial mathematical challenges.
After performing an Euler-type self-similar transformation to
CNS
,
the structure of the equations changes fundamentally.
In particular, the original viscous terms are transformed into a degenerate nonlinear elliptic operator, together with additional coupling terms between the velocity field and the density.
As a result, the standard energy methods and uniform elliptic estimates are no longer directly
applicable.
To overcome these difficulties, the analysis requires a delicate interplay of
carefully designed weighted estimates across different spatial regimes.

The main contribution of this paper is to show that the above intuition
fails in a certain physical parameter regime.
More precisely, when the viscosity exponent
\delta
is smaller than a threshold value

\delta^{*}(\gamma)
,
depending on the adiabatic exponent
\gamma
, the nonlinear viscous terms are not strong enough
to counterbalance the convective effects, leading to finite-time implosion.
Our main result on the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.6
)
can be stated as follows:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
be the
C^{\infty}
self-similar profile
solving

\begin{cases}(\Lambda-1)\overline{Q}+(y+\overline{U})\cdot\nabla_{y}\overline{Q}+\alpha\overline{Q}\,\mathrm{div}_{y}\overline{U}=0,\\[6.0pt]
(\Lambda-1)\overline{U}+(y+\overline{U})\cdot\nabla_{y}\overline{U}+\alpha\overline{Q}\,\nabla_{y}\overline{Q}=0,\end{cases}

(1.16)

which is obtained in
Lemma
B.2

(
see
Appendix
B
)
, and let

\delta^{*}(\gamma)
be a constant given by

\displaystyle\delta^{*}(\gamma)=\begin{cases}\frac{\gamma+1}{4}-\frac{\sqrt{2(\gamma-1)}}{2}&\text{for}\,\,\,\,1<\gamma<\frac{5}{3},\\[6.0pt]
\frac{1-(2\sqrt{3}-3)\gamma}{2(3-\sqrt{3})}&\text{for}\,\,\,\,\frac{5}{3}\leq\gamma<1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}.\end{cases}

(1.17)

If parameters
(\gamma,\delta)
satisfy
any one of the following conditions
(P_{1})
–
(P_{2})
:

(\rm P_{1})

1<\gamma<1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}},\quad\,0<\delta<\frac{1}{2},\quad\,\delta_{\rm dis}=\frac{(1-\delta)(\Lambda-1)}{\alpha}+\Lambda-2>0,

(1.18)

where
\Lambda\in(1,\Lambda^{*}(\gamma))
is the scaling parameter introduced in
Lemma
B.1
;

(\rm P_{2})

\gamma\in(1,1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}})\setminus\mathcal{J},\quad 0<\delta<\delta^{*}(\gamma),

(1.19)

where the set
\mathcal{J}

(
possibly empty
)

contains at most countably many points excluding
\{\frac{5}{3},\frac{7}{5}\}
and is introduced in
Lemma
B.1
,

then, for two constants
T,\sigma>0
sufficiently small,
there are
C^{\infty}
initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
with
\rho_{0}>\sigma
such that the corresponding smooth solution
(\rho,u)(t,x)
of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)
–
(
1.6
) blows up at time
T
:
for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\quad\ \ \ \ \lim_{t\to T}\rho(t,0)=\infty,\qquad\lim_{t\to T}\sup_{|x|\leq\varepsilon}|u(t,x)|=\infty.

(1.20)

More precisely, for any fixed
y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to T}\,\big(\alpha^{-1}\Lambda(T-t)^{1-\frac{1}{\Lambda}}\big)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\rho(t,(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}y)

\displaystyle=\overline{Q}(|y|)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}},

(1.21)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to T}\,\Lambda(T-t)^{1-\frac{1}{\Lambda}}u(t,(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}y)

\displaystyle=\overline{U}(|y|).

Moreover, there exists a finite-codimension set of initial data satisfying the above conclusions
(
see
Appendix
D

for more details
)
.

Furthermore, we consider the period problem in a torus of period
10
:

\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}:=(\mathbb{R}/10\mathbb{Z})^{3}

with the fundamental domain
[-5,5)^{3}
, and take
0
as the center of the torus (without loss of generality, by scaling).
The same conclusion also holds for the periodic problem in
\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}
:

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\rho u)=0,\\[6.0pt]
\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\rho u)+\mathrm{div}_{x}(\rho u\otimes u)+\nabla_{x}P=\mathrm{div}_{x}\mathbb{S},\\[6.0pt]
(\rho,u)(0,x)=(\rho_{0},u_{0})(x)\qquad\,\,\text{for $x\in\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3}$},\end{cases}

(1.22)

which can be stated as follows:

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
be the
C^{\infty}
self-similar profile
solving (
1.16
),
which is obtained in
Lemma
B.2

(
see
Appendix
B
);

and let
\delta^{*}(\gamma)
be a constant given by (
1.17
).
If parameters
(\gamma,\delta)
satisfy condition

(P_{1})
or condition
(P_{2})
shown in (
1.18
)–(
1.19
),
then for two constants
T,\sigma>0
sufficiently small,
there are
C^{\infty}
initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
with
\rho_{0}>\sigma
such that the corresponding smooth solution
(\rho,u)(t,x)
of problem (
1.22
) blows up at time
T
:

for any
\varepsilon>0
, (
1.20
) holds.
More precisely, for any fixed
y\in\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3}
, (
1.21
) holds.
Moreover, there exists a finite-codimension set of initial data satisfying the above conclusions
(
see
Appendix
D

for more details
)
.

In what follows, we provide a detailed proof for Theorem
1.1
.
The proof of Theorem
1.2
follows similar arguments, and we therefore only include
some remarks on the proof of this situation at the end of this paper.

We now make some remarks on the results in Theorems
1.1
–
1.2
.

Remark 1.1
.

We make some remarks on the set
\mathcal{J}
appearing in condition
(P_{2})
of
Theorem
1.1
.
In
[
37
]
, it is shown that, for
\gamma\in(1,\infty)\setminus\mathcal{J}_{0}
,
there exists a discrete sequence
\{\Lambda_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
satisfying (
1.13
) such that system (
1.15
) admits a smooth, spherically symmetric profile
(\overline{Q}_{n},\overline{U}_{n})
for each
\Lambda_{n}
, where
\mathcal{J}_{0}
is a
(
possibly empty
)
exceptional set containing at most countably many points
;

see
Theorem 1.1 in
[
37
]
.
Moreover, it was shown in
[
43
]
and
[
2
]
that such sequences
\{\Lambda_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}

also exist for
\gamma=\frac{5}{3}
and
\gamma=\frac{7}{5}
, respectively.
Based on the arguments presented in
§8
, we can show that
\frac{5}{3},\frac{7}{5}\notin\mathcal{J}
.

Remark 1.2
.

The constant,
\delta^{*}(\gamma)
, in
Theorems
1.1
–
1.2

has the following asymptotic behavior
:

\delta^{*}(\gamma)\to\frac{1}{2}\quad\text{as $\gamma\to 1$},\qquad\quad\delta^{*}(\gamma)\to 0\quad\text{as $\gamma\to 1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}$}.

Remark 1.3
.

It follows from the properties of profile
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
shown in
Lemma
B.2
that
\overline{Q}(0)>0
, and there exists some finite
y^{*}
with
|\overline{U}(y^{*})|>0
. Then (
1.21
) implies that, for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\lim_{t\to T}\rho(t,0)=\infty,\qquad\lim_{t\to T}\sup_{|x|\leq\varepsilon}|u(t,x)|\geq\lim_{t\to T}\frac{1}{\Lambda}(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}-1}|\overline{U}(y^{*})|=\infty.

Remark 1.4
.

Theorems
1.1
–
1.2
can be applied to several physical models. For example, we consider a class of barotropic cut-off inverse power force models for which
\delta=(\frac{1}{2}+b)(\gamma-1)
with
b\in[0,\infty)
.
We list below three representative choices of parameter
b
:

(i)

for rigid elastic spherical molecules:
b=0
and
\delta=\frac{1}{2}(\gamma-1)
,
it is required that
1<\gamma<7-4\sqrt{2}
;

(ii)

for Maxwellian molecules:
b=\frac{1}{2}
and
\delta=\gamma-1
, it is required that
1<\gamma<1+\frac{2}{9}
;

(iii)

for the ionized gas:
b=2
and
\delta=\frac{5}{2}(\gamma-1)
,
it is required that
1<\gamma<1+\frac{22-4\sqrt{10}}{81}
.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows: In §
2
, we introduce the notations, present a new reformulation of (
1.1
), and outline the proof of the main results.
In §
3
, we establish the desired local-in-time well-posedness for the 3-D reformulated problem.
In §
4
–§
7
, we establish the global-in-time well-posedness
for the reformulated
system with smooth initial data chosen from a finite-codimensional manifold by a bootstrap argument.
In §
8
, we analyze the singularity formation for
CNS
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
as stated in Theorem
1.1
.
In §
9
, we provide some remarks on the proof of Theorem
1.2
,
concerning the development of implosions of solutions of the periodic problems.
Finally, in Appendices A-D, we present several auxiliary lemmas used throughout the analysis,
as well as some properties of the self-similar profile
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
of the steady Euler equations, as verified in
[
2
,
5
,
37
,
43
]
.

2.
Notations, Reformulations, and Outline of the Proof

In this section, we first introduce the notations in §
2.1
,
which will be frequently used throughout this paper.
Next, in §
2.2
, we present a suitable reformulation of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.6
), which renders the problem amenable to analysis.
Finally, in §
2.3
, we outline the main strategies and key new ideas
of our analysis, based on this reformulation.

2.1.
Notations

The following notations will be frequently used in this paper.

2.1.1.
Notations on coordinates and operators

•

Let
f
be any function defined on a measurable subset of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with independent variables
z=(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})^{\top}
. For any integer
k\geq 1
and an ordered multi-index

\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\cdots,\beta_{k})
with

\beta_{i}\in\{1,2,3\}
for
i=1,\cdots,k
, we define the length of
\beta
by
|\beta|=k
, and denote

\begin{split}&\partial^{z}_{\beta}f=\partial_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\cdots,\beta_{k}}f=\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial z_{\beta_{1}}\partial{z_{\beta_{2}}}\cdots\partial{z_{\beta_{k}}}}f,\\
&\nabla_{z}f=(\partial_{z_{1}}f,\partial_{z_{2}}f,\partial_{z_{3}}f)^{\top},\quad\Delta_{z}f=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial^{2}_{z_{i}}}f,\\
&\nabla^{k}_{z}f\ \text{denotes one generic}\ \partial^{z}_{\beta}f\ \text{with}\ |\beta|=k\text{ for integer }k\geq 2,\\
&|\nabla^{k}_{z}f|=\Big(\sum_{|\beta|=k}|\partial^{z}_{\beta}f|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad\text{ for integer }k\geq 1.\end{split}

Moreover, we define the
\beta
–related, ordered multi-index
\beta^{(j)}
(
j=1,\cdots,k
):

\beta^{(j)}=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\cdots,\beta_{j-1},\beta_{j+1},\cdots,\beta_{k}),

(2.1)

and then

\begin{split}\partial^{z}_{\beta^{(j)}}f=&\frac{\partial^{k-1}}{\partial z_{\beta_{1}}\partial z_{\beta_{2}}\cdots\partial z_{\beta_{j-1}}\partial z_{\beta_{j+1}}\cdots\partial z_{\beta_{k}}}f\quad\text{for}~j=1,\cdots,k.\end{split}

(2.2)

•

Let
f=(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})^{\top}:\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}

(
\Omega
is a measurable set) be a vector function with independent
variables
z=(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})^{\top}
and
\mathcal{X}\in\{\partial^{z}_{\beta},\partial^{z}_{\beta^{(j)}},\Delta_{z},\nabla_{z}^{k}\}
.
Then, for any integer
k\geq 1
and an ordered multi-index

\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\cdots,\beta_{k})
with

\beta_{i}\in\{1,2,3\}
for
i=1,\cdots,k
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}f=\big(\mathcal{X}f_{1},\mathcal{X}f_{2},\mathcal{X}f_{3}\big)^{\top},\ \quad\nabla_{z}f=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{z_{1}}f_{1}&\partial_{z_{2}}f_{1}&\partial_{z_{3}}f_{1}\\[5.69054pt]
\partial_{z_{1}}f_{2}&\partial_{z_{2}}f_{2}&\partial_{z_{3}}f_{2}\\[5.69054pt]
\partial_{z_{1}}f_{3}&\partial_{z_{2}}f_{3}&\partial_{z_{3}}f_{3}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle|\nabla^{k}_{z}f|=\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sum_{|\beta|=k}|\partial_{\beta}^{z}f_{i}|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Moreover, it follows that
\mathrm{div}_{z}\,f=\partial_{z_{1}}f_{1}+\partial_{z_{2}}f_{2}+\partial_{z_{3}}f_{3}
.

In particular, for the derivatives with respect to the self-similar spatial
coordinates
y=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})^{\top}
introduced in (
1.11
),
we use the notation:

(\partial_{\beta},\partial_{\beta^{(j)}},\nabla,\Delta,\nabla^{k},\mathrm{div})=(\partial^{y}_{\beta},\partial^{y}_{\beta^{(j)}},\nabla_{y},\Delta_{y},\nabla_{y}^{k},\mathrm{div}_{y}).

•

Let
\beta
be a multi-index, and let
\mathcal{A}
be a linear operator.
For any sufficiently smooth function
f
defined on a measurable subset of
\mathbb{R}^{3}

with independent variables
z=(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})^{\top}
, we define the commutator between

\partial^{z}_{\beta}
and
\mathcal{A}
by

[\partial^{z}_{\beta},\,\mathcal{A}]f:=\partial^{z}_{\beta}(\mathcal{A}f)-\mathcal{A}(\partial^{z}_{\beta}f).

Additionally, if
f=(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})^{\top}:\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
(
\Omega
is a measurable set) is a vector function with the independent variables
z=(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})^{\top}
, then

[\partial_{\beta}^{z},\,\mathcal{A}]f=([\partial_{\beta}^{z},\,\mathcal{A}]f_{1},[\partial_{\beta}^{z},\,\mathcal{A}]f_{2},[\partial_{\beta}^{z},\,\mathcal{A}]f_{3})^{\top}.

2.1.2.
Notations on function spaces

•

For
R>0
, we denote by

B(0,R):=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\,|z|<R\}

the open ball in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
centered at the origin with radius
R
,
B^{c}(0,R):=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\,|z|\geq R\}
the corresponding exterior region of
B(0,R)
, and
\partial B(0,R):=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\,|z|=R\}
the boundary of
B(0,R)
.

•

For any (scalar or vector valued) functions
f
and
g
defined in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and for some function spaces
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
, if the independent variables of
(f,g)
are
z=(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})^{\top}
, we adopt the following simplified notation for the standard Sobolev spaces:

\begin{split}&\quad\ \ \|f\|_{H^{s}}=\|f\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})},\ \ \|f\|_{L^{p}}=\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})},\ \ \|f\|_{X_{1}\cap X_{2}}=\|f\|_{X_{1}}+\|f\|_{X_{2}},\ \ \\[6.0pt]
&\quad\ \ \ D^{k,r}=\big\{f\in L^{1}_{\rm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):\,\|f\|_{D^{k,r}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}=\|\nabla^{k}_{z}f\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}<\infty\big\},\ \ D^{k}=D^{k,2},\\[6.0pt]
&\quad\ \ \ \|f\|_{D^{k,r}}=\|f\|_{D^{k,r}(\mathbb{R}^{3})},\quad\|(f,g)\|_{X_{1}}=\|f\|_{X_{1}}+\|g\|_{X_{1}}.\end{split}

•

For an open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
and any integer
m\geq 0
, we define

\displaystyle\quad\ \ H_{0}^{m}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}):=H_{0}^{m}(\Omega),\quad H_{0}^{m}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}):=\{\,f=(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})^{\top}:\,f_{i}\in H_{0}^{m}(\Omega),\ i=1,2,3\},

where
H_{0}^{m}(\Omega)
denotes the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
in
H^{m}(\Omega)
.

•

We define the following function space:

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle:=H_{0}^{m}(B(0,3C_{0});\mathbb{R})\times H_{0}^{m}(B(0,3C_{0});\mathbb{R}^{3})

(2.3)

\displaystyle~=\{(Q(z),U(z)):\,Q(z)\in H_{0}^{m}(B(0,3C_{0});\mathbb{R}),U(z)\in H_{0}^{m}(B(0,3C_{0});\mathbb{R}^{3})\},

where
m
is a sufficiently large positive integer that will be determined later (see Lemma
C.3
in Appendix
C
), and
C_{0}
is a sufficiently large constant that will be determined later (see Lemma
4.1
).
For
(Q_{1},U_{1})
,
(Q_{2},U_{2})\in X
, the corresponding inner product is given by

\begin{split}&\langle(Q_{1},U_{1}),(Q_{2},U_{2})\rangle_{X}\\
\ &=\int_{B(0,3C_{0})}\big(\nabla^{m}_{z}Q_{1}\cdot\nabla^{m}_{z}Q_{2}+\nabla^{m}_{z}U_{1}\cdot\nabla^{m}_{z}U_{2}+Q_{1}Q_{2}+U_{1}\cdot U_{2}\big)\,\text{d}z\\
&=\int_{B(0,3C_{0})}\big(\sum_{|\beta|=m}(\partial_{\beta}^{z}Q_{1}\partial_{\beta}^{z}Q_{2}+\partial_{\beta}^{z}U_{1}\cdot\partial_{\beta}^{z}U_{2})+Q_{1}Q_{2}+U_{1}\cdot U_{2}\big)\,\text{d}z.\end{split}

We obtain the inherited norm:

\begin{split}\|(Q,U)\|_{X}^{2}&=\int_{B(0,3C_{0})}\big(|\nabla^{m}_{z}Q|^{2}+|\nabla^{m}_{z}U|^{2}+Q^{2}+|U|^{2}\big)\,\text{d}z\\
&=\int_{B(0,3C_{0})}\big(\sum_{|\beta|=m}(|\partial_{\beta}^{z}Q|^{2}+|\partial_{\beta}^{z}U|^{2})+Q^{2}+|U|^{2}\big)\,\text{d}z.\end{split}

(2.4)

•

We recall that the Hilbert space
X
in (
2.3
) admits the invariant decomposition

X=V_{\mathrm{sta}}\oplus V_{\mathrm{uns}},

(2.5)

where
V_{\mathrm{uns}}
is finite-dimensional and consists of smooth functions.
This decomposition is established in Lemma
C.1
in Appendix
C
.
We denote by

P_{\mathrm{sta}}:X\to V_{\mathrm{sta}},\qquad P_{\mathrm{uns}}:X\to V_{\mathrm{uns}},

the corresponding projections onto the stable subspace
V_{\mathrm{sta}}
and the unstable subspace
V_{\mathrm{uns}}
, respectively.

2.1.3.
Other notations

•

The
Japanese bracket

\langle\bullet\rangle
is defined by
\langle z\rangle:=\sqrt{1+|z|^{2}}\text{ for }z\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

•

For any integrable function
f(z)
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f\,{\rm d}z=\int f\,{\rm d}z.

•

We write
\mathsf{a}\ll\mathsf{b}
if
\mathsf{a}
is far smaller than
\mathsf{b}
,
and write

\mathsf{a}\gg\mathsf{b}
if
\mathsf{a}
is far larger than
\mathsf{b}
.

•

The function,
\mathscr{X}(z)
, defined in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
denotes a smooth spherically symmetric cutoff function satisfying

\operatorname{supp}\mathscr{X}\subset B(0,1),\qquad\,\mathscr{X}(z)=1\,\,\text{ on $B(0,\tfrac{1}{2})$},\qquad\,|\nabla_{z}\mathscr{X}|\leq 10.

(2.6)

For convenience, we define
\widehat{X}(z,\tau):=\mathscr{X}(e^{-\tau}z)
.

•

We introduce two smooth, spherically symmetric cutoff functions. Let
\chi_{1}(\varsigma):\mathbb{R}\rightarrow[0,1]
be supported on
[-\frac{3}{2}C_{0},\frac{3}{2}C_{0}]
and satisfy
\chi_{1}(\varsigma)=1
for
|\varsigma|\leq C_{0}
, where
C_{0}
is a sufficiently large constant that will be determined later (see Lemma
4.1
). Let
\chi_{2}(\varsigma):\mathbb{R}\rightarrow[0,1]
be supported on
[-\frac{5}{2}C_{0},\frac{5}{2}C_{0}]
and satisfy
\chi_{2}(\varsigma)=1
for
|\varsigma|\leq 2C_{0}
, with
\chi_{2}(\varsigma)>0
in
(-\frac{5}{2}C_{0},\frac{5}{2}C_{0})
.

Figure 1.
The graph of
J(1-\chi_{1})
and
\chi_{2}
, where
J
is a sufficient large constant determined in Lemma
C.3
.

•

We also employ a spherically symmetric weight function
\phi\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
defined as

\displaystyle\phi(z)=\begin{cases}1&\text{ for }|z|\leq R_{0},\\[6.0pt]
\frac{|z|^{2(1-\eta)}}{2R_{0}^{2(1-\eta)}}&\text{ for }|z|\geq 4R_{0}.\end{cases}

(2.7)

Here
0<\eta\ll 1
is a small constant to be fixed later in the bootstrap argument (see §
6
),
and constant
R_{0}>0
will be chosen later (see (
4.3
)).

2.2.
Reformulation

Throughout §
2
–§
9
,
C\geq 1
denotes a generic constant depending only on
(\Lambda,\gamma,\delta,a_{1},a_{2})
, and
C(\nu_{1},\cdots\!,\nu_{k})\geq 1
denotes a generic constant depending on
C

and parameters
\nu_{1},\cdots\!,\nu_{k}
, which may be different at each occurrence.

2.2.1.
Reformulation based on the self-similar scaling

First, denote
\alpha=\frac{\gamma-1}{2}
and
c=\frac{1}{\alpha}\rho^{\alpha}
.
Then the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.6
) can be rewritten as

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}c=-u\cdot\nabla_{x}c-\alpha c\,\mathrm{div}_{x}u,\\[4.0pt]
\partial_{t}u=-u\cdot\nabla_{x}u-\alpha c\nabla_{x}c+\alpha^{\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}}c^{\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}}L_{x}u+\frac{\delta}{\alpha}\alpha^{\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}}c^{\frac{\delta-1-\alpha}{\alpha}}\nabla_{x}c\cdot\mathbb{D}_{x}(u),\\[4.0pt]
(c,u)(0,x)=(c_{0},u_{0})(x):=(\alpha^{-1}\rho^{\alpha}_{0},u_{0})(x)\qquad\ \ \text{for $x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}$},\\[4.0pt]
\displaystyle(c,u)(t,x)\to(\bar{c},0):=(\alpha^{-1}\bar{\rho}^{\alpha},0)\qquad\ \ \qquad\qquad\text{as $|x|\to\infty$\, for $t\geq 0$},\end{cases}

(2.8)

where

L_{x}u=a_{1}\Delta_{x}u+(a_{1}+a_{2})\nabla_{x}\mathrm{div}_{x}u,\quad\mathbb{D}_{x}(u)=a_{1}\big(\nabla_{x}u+(\nabla_{x}u)^{\top}\big)+a_{2}\,\mathrm{div}_{x}u\,\mathbb{I}_{3}.

(2.9)

Second, for fixed constants
T>0
and
\Lambda>1
, we introduce the self-similar scaling:

\tau=-\frac{\log(T-t)}{\Lambda},\quad y=\frac{x}{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}}=e^{\tau}x,\quad\tau_{0}=-\frac{\log T}{\Lambda},

(2.10)

and

\displaystyle c(x,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}-1}}{\Lambda}Q(-\frac{\log(T-t)}{\Lambda},\frac{x}{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}}),

(2.11)

\displaystyle u(x,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}-1}}{\Lambda}U(-\frac{\log(T-t)}{\Lambda},\frac{x}{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}}).

Then the reformulated system for
(Q,U)
is given by

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}Q=-(\Lambda-1)Q-(y+U)\cdot\nabla Q-\alpha Q\,\mathrm{div}U,\\[4.0pt]
\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}U=-(\Lambda-1)U-(y+U)\cdot\nabla U-\alpha Q\nabla Q\\[4.0pt]
\qquad\quad\,+\,C_{\rm{dis}}e^{-\delta_{\rm{dis}}\tau}\big(Q^{\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}}L(U)+\frac{\delta}{\alpha}Q^{\frac{\delta-1-\alpha}{\alpha}}\nabla Q\cdot\mathbb{D}(U)\big),\end{cases}

(2.12)

where

L(U)=a_{1}\Delta U+(a_{1}+a_{2})\nabla\mathrm{div}U,\quad\,\,\,\mathbb{D}(U)=a_{1}\big(\nabla U+(\nabla U)^{\top}\big)+a_{2}\,\mathrm{div}U\,\mathbb{I}_{3},

(2.13)

and constants
C_{\rm{dis}}
and
\delta_{\rm{dis}}
are defined by

C_{\rm{dis}}=\alpha^{\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}}\Lambda^{\frac{1-\delta+\alpha}{\alpha}}>0,\quad\quad\delta_{\rm{dis}}=\frac{(1-\delta)(\Lambda-1)}{\alpha}+(\Lambda-2)>0.

(2.14)

Next, we consider the Cauchy problem for (
2.12
)–(
2.14
) with the initial and far-field conditions:

\displaystyle(Q,U)(\tau_{0},y)

\displaystyle=(Q_{0},U_{0})(y):=\big(\Lambda e^{-(\Lambda-1)\tau_{0}}c_{0},\,\Lambda e^{-(\Lambda-1)\tau_{0}}u_{0}\big)(e^{-\tau_{0}}y)\quad

\displaystyle\text{for}\,\,\,y\in\mathbb{R}^{3},

(2.15)

\displaystyle(Q,U)(\tau,y)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\big(Q^{*},0\big):=\big(\Lambda e^{-(\Lambda-1)\tau}\bar{c},\,0\big)\quad\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{as }|y|\to\infty\

\displaystyle\text{for}\,\,\,\tau\geq\tau_{0}.

(2.16)

Remark 2.1
.

The explicit form of the threshold exponent (
1.17
), derived from the condition that
\delta_{\rm{dis}}>0
, is imposed to guarantee that, after the reformulation, the dissipative terms exhibit the required temporal decay, thereby allowing
CNS
to develop an implosion analogous to that of inviscid flows.

2.2.2.
Reformulation based on
C^{\infty}
smooth spherically symmetric profiles of the steady Euler equations

Let
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
be the
C^{\infty}
self-similar profile solving (
1.16
) obtained
in Lemma
B.2
(see Appendix
B
)
with the scaling parameter
\Lambda\in(1,\Lambda^{*}(\gamma))
introduced in Lemma
B.1
.
Since
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})

is spherically symmetric, then there exists a scalar function
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
such that

\overline{Q}(y)=\overline{Q}(r),\quad\overline{U}(y)=\overline{\mathcal{U}}(r)\frac{y}{r}\qquad\,\,\mbox{for $r=|y|$}.

In fact, in the proof for Theorem
1.1
,

•

the study on the development of implosions for the original Cauchy problem of
CNS
can be reduced to a stability analysis around the cutoff self-similar profile
(\widehat{X}\overline{Q},\widehat{X}\overline{U})
,

where
\widehat{X}
denotes the cutoff function introduced in (
2.6
).
Motivated by this viewpoint, the key issue is to analyze the mathematical structure satisfied by the corresponding perturbation:

\widetilde{Q}=Q-\widehat{X}\overline{Q},\quad\quad\widetilde{U}=U-\widehat{X}\overline{U}.

(2.17)

According to (
1.16
) and (
2.12
),

(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})
satisfies the following
equations:

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\widetilde{Q}=

\displaystyle\underbrace{-(\Lambda-1)\widetilde{Q}-(y+\widehat{X}\overline{U})\cdot\nabla\widetilde{Q}-\alpha(\widehat{X}\overline{Q})\,\mathrm{div}\,\widetilde{U}-\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla(\widehat{X}\overline{Q})-\alpha\widetilde{Q}\,\mathrm{div}(\widehat{X}\overline{U})}_{\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}^{e}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})}

\displaystyle\underbrace{-\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{Q}-\alpha\widetilde{Q}\,\mathrm{div}\,\widetilde{U}}_{\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})}

(2.18)

\displaystyle\underbrace{-(\widehat{X}^{2}-\widehat{X})\overline{U}\cdot\nabla\overline{Q}-(1+\alpha)\widehat{X}\overline{U}\cdot\nabla\widehat{X}\,\overline{Q}-(\widehat{X}^{2}-\widehat{X})\overline{Q}\,\mathrm{div}\,\overline{U}}_{\mathcal{E}_{\zeta}},

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\widetilde{U}=

\displaystyle\underbrace{-(\Lambda-1)\widetilde{U}-(y+\widehat{X}\overline{U})\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U}-\alpha(\widehat{X}\overline{Q})\nabla\widetilde{Q}-\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla(\widehat{X}\overline{U})-\alpha\widetilde{Q}\nabla(\widehat{X}\overline{Q})}_{\mathcal{L}_{u}^{e}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})}

\displaystyle\underbrace{-\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U}-\alpha\widetilde{Q}\nabla\widetilde{Q}}_{\mathcal{N}_{u}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})}

(2.19)

\displaystyle\underbrace{-(\widehat{X}^{2}-\widehat{X})\overline{U}\cdot\nabla\overline{U}-\widehat{X}\overline{U}\cdot\nabla\widehat{X}\,\overline{U}-\alpha(\widehat{X}^{2}-\widehat{X})\,\overline{Q}\nabla\overline{Q}-\alpha\widehat{X}\overline{Q}\,\nabla\widehat{X}\overline{Q}}_{\mathcal{E}_{u}}

\displaystyle\underbrace{+C_{\rm{dis}}e^{-\delta_{\rm{dis}}\tau}\big(Q^{\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}}L(U)+\frac{\delta}{\alpha}Q^{\frac{\delta-1-\alpha}{\alpha}}\nabla Q\cdot\mathbb{D}(U)\big)}_{\mathcal{F}_{\rm{dis}}},

where we have used the fact that
\partial\tau\widehat{X}=-y\cdot\nabla\widehat{X}
. Here

•

\mathcal{L}^{e}=(\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}^{e},\mathcal{L}_{u}^{e})
denotes the linear part in
the time evolution equations of
(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})
;

•

\mathcal{N}=(\mathcal{N}_{\zeta},\mathcal{N}_{u})
collects the nonlinear terms (at least quadratic in

(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})
);

•

\mathcal{E}=(\mathcal{E}_{\zeta},\mathcal{E}_{u})
contains all the terms
that depend only on the known
profile and

time-independent coefficients (
e.g.

\widehat{X}
,
\overline{Q}
,
\overline{U}
);

•

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{dis}}
denotes the exponentially decaying
dissipative term produced by the regularization.

The properties of the linear operator
\mathcal{L}^{e}
play an important role in our analysis.
In Appendix
C
, we will see that
\mathcal{L}^{e}
exhibits some dissipative properties in bounded spatial regions.
To capture this feature and facilitate precise estimates, we fix a constant

C_{0}>0
, depending only on the self-similar profile

(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
and
\Lambda
, and then decompose
\mathbb{R}^{3}
into
B(0,C_{0})
and
B^{c}(0,C_{0})
.

However, such dissipative properties fail in the far-fields. To further localize the analysis, we define the truncated linear operator
\mathcal{L}:=(\mathcal{L}_{\zeta},\mathcal{L}_{u})
by

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})=\chi_{2}\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}^{e}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})-J(1-\chi_{1})\widetilde{Q},\\[6.0pt]
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{u}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})=\chi_{2}\mathcal{L}_{u}^{e}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})-J(1-\chi_{1})\widetilde{U},\end{cases}

(2.20)

where
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
are two smooth cutoff functions defined in §
2.1.3
and
J
is a sufficiently large constant defined in Lemma
C.3
so as to enforce the uniform dissipation of
\mathcal{L}
in the exterior region
B^{c}(0,C_{0})
. According to the definition of
\mathcal{L}
in (
2.20
),
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}^{e}
in
B(0,C_{0})
.

As shown in
[
5
]
, for
m
determined in Lemma
C.3
, in the Hilbert
space
H_{0}^{m}(B(0,3C_{0});\mathbb{R})\times H_{0}^{m}(B(0,3C_{0});\mathbb{R}^{3})

defined in (
2.3
) based on the choice of
(m,C_{0})
, operator
\mathcal{L}

is maximally dissipative, modulo a finite-rank perturbation corresponding to the unstable modes.

Now we are ready to state the desired global stability theory of problem (
2.12
)–(
2.16
).

Theorem 2.1
.

We assume that
1<\gamma<1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}
,
\Lambda\in(1,\Lambda^{*}(\gamma))
is the scaling parameter introduced in
Lemma
B.1
such that there exists a smooth, spherically symmetric self-similar profile
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
solving (
1.16
),

\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
satisfies
\delta_{\rm dis}>0
,

(m,J)
are two sufficiently large constants determined in
Lemma
C.3
,
and
C_{0}>1
is a sufficiently large constant depending on profile
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
and parameter
\Lambda
.
Then there exist positive constants
\nu_{1},E
, and
K
, which depend only on
(m,J)

and satisfy

K\geq 4,\qquad\nu_{1}\ll\frac{1}{E}\ll\frac{1}{K}\ll 1,

such that, if the initial data function
(Q_{0},U_{0})
satisfies

\inf_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}Q_{0}>0,\quad Q_{0}-Q^{*}\in H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad U_{0}\in H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),

and

\max\big\{\|Q_{0}-\widehat{X}\overline{Q}\|_{L^{\infty}},\ \|U_{0}-\widehat{X}\overline{U}\|_{L^{\infty}}\big\}\leq\nu_{1},\quad Q_{0}\geq\frac{\nu_{1}}{2},\quad\langle y\rangle^{\Lambda}(|\nabla Q_{0}|+|\nabla U_{0}|)<\infty,

(2.21)

\max\big\{\|P_{\rm sta}(\chi_{2}(Q_{0}-\widehat{X}\overline{Q}))\|_{X},\ \|P_{\rm sta}(\chi_{2}(U_{0}-\widehat{X}\overline{U}))\|_{X}\}\leq\nu_{1},

(2.22)

E_{K}(\tau_{0})=\int\big(|\nabla^{K}Q(\tau_{0},y)|^{2}+|\nabla^{K}U(\tau_{0},y)|^{2}\big)\phi^{K}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\frac{E}{2},

(2.23)

where the function space
X
is defined in (
2.3
) based on the choice of
(m,C_{0})
,
and the weight function
\phi\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is defined in (
2.7
),
then the Cauchy problem (
2.12
)
–
(
2.16
) admits a unique global-in-time smooth solution
(Q,U)
satisfying

\begin{split}&\inf_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}Q(\tau,y)>0,\quad Q-Q^{*}\in C([\tau_{0},\tau];H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),\\
&\partial_{\tau}Q\in C([\tau_{0},\tau];H^{K-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),\ \ U\in C([\tau_{0},\tau];H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))\cap L^{2}([\tau_{0},\tau];H^{K+1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),\\[2.0pt]
&\partial_{\tau}U\in C([\tau_{0},\tau];H^{K-2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))\cap L^{2}([\tau_{0},\tau];H^{K-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),\end{split}

(2.24)

and

\max\big\{\|Q-\widehat{X}\overline{Q}\|_{L^{\infty}},\ \|U-\widehat{X}\overline{U}\|_{L^{\infty}}\big\}\leq\frac{\nu_{0}}{100}e^{-\varepsilon(\tau-\tau_{0})},\qquad E_{K}(\tau)\leq E,

(2.25)

for all
\tau>\tau_{0}
, where
\nu_{0}>0
is a sufficiently small positive constant depending only on
E
, and
\varepsilon>0
is a small positive constant depending only on
\delta_{\rm dis}
, so that they satisfy

\nu_{0}^{3/2}\ll\nu_{1}\ll\nu_{0}\ll\frac{1}{E}\ll\frac{1}{K}\ll\frac{1}{m}\ll\varepsilon\ll 1.

Moreover, there exists a finite-codimension set of initial data satisfying the above conditions
(
see
Appendix
D

for more details
)
.

Remark 2.2

(Choice and Hierarchy of Parameters)
.

Throughout the proof of
Theorem
2.1
, the parameters are chosen in the following order
and satisfy the relations below
:

(i)

The constant
\varepsilon>0
depends only on
\delta_{\rm dis}
and is chosen such that

\varepsilon<\frac{1}{4}\,\delta_{\rm dis}=O(1).

(ii)

The integer
K
is chosen sufficiently large so that

K>\max\{3^{m}\,C(J,m),\,500\},

where
J
and
m
are fixed as in
Lemma
C.3
.

(iii)

The energy bound
E
is taken large enough to satisfy

E>C3^{2K^{2}}.

(iv)

The constant
\nu_{0}>0
is chosen sufficiently small, depending on
E
, such that

\nu_{0}<E^{-1}.

(v)

The constant
\nu_{1}>0
is chosen to satisfy

\nu_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}}\ll\nu_{1}\ll\nu_{0};

for instance, one may take
\nu_{1}=\nu_{0}^{\frac{4}{3}}
.

It is worth noting that all parameters are chosen independently of time
\tau
.

2.3.
Outline of the Proof

As mentioned in §
2.2.2
,

•

the study on the finite-time implosion of the smooth solution
(\rho,u)(t,x)
of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.6
) for the original
CNS
can be reduced to a global-in-time stability analysis around the cutoff self-similar profile
(\widehat{X}\overline{Q},\widehat{X}\overline{U})(y)
for solution
(Q,U)(\tau,y)
of the reformulated problem (
2.12
)–(
2.16
).

Thus, in order to prove Theorem
1.1
,

(i)

First, based on the self-similar solutions to the steady Euler equations, we apply the same self-similar scaling to
CNS
,
thus obtaining the reformulated system (
2.12
).

(ii)

Second, we establish a global-in-time stability analysis around profile
(\widehat{X}\overline{Q},\widehat{X}\overline{U})

for solution
(Q,U)
of the reformulated problem (
2.12
)–(
2.16
) (see Theorem
2.1
). Moreover, we can recover a solution
(\rho,u)
to
CNS
(
1.1
) on
[0,T)
(see §
3
–§
7
).

(iii)

Finally, based on the global stability result obtained above,
in the original
(t,x)
–variables, solution
(\rho,u)
to (
1.1
) satisfies the perturbative bounds:

\displaystyle\Big|\Lambda(T-t)^{1-\frac{1}{\Lambda}}c(t,x)-\mathscr{X}(x)\overline{Q}(\frac{x}{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}})\Big|\leq\frac{\nu_{0}}{100}\Big(\frac{T-t}{T}\Big)^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\Lambda}},

\displaystyle\Big|\Lambda(T-t)^{1-\frac{1}{\Lambda}}u(t,x)-\mathscr{X}(x)\overline{U}(\frac{x}{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}})\Big|\leq\frac{\nu_{0}}{100}\Big(\frac{T-t}{T}\Big)^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\Lambda}},

for all
(t,x)\in(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, where

(c,u)
=
(\alpha^{-1}\rho^{\alpha},u)
.
These estimates show that the solution to
CNS
remains a small perturbation of the self-similar Euler profile, thereby showing the implosions of the smooths solutions to the original
CNS
(
1.1
) in finite time and then completing the proof of Theorem
1.1
(see §
8
).

According to the above discussion, the main part of the proof for Theorem
1.1

is to prove Theorem
2.1
.
In fact, for establishing such kind of global-in-time stability results
CNS
,
motivated by the classical work in Matsumura–Nishida
[
36
]
, Wang–Xu
[
48
]
, among others,
the following two points are crucial:
Within the life span of the solutions of problem (
2.12
)–(
2.16
),

(i)

Q
is uniformly lower and upper bounded;

(ii)

(Q,U)
can maintain the smallness condition as that of the initial data.

Such information can usually be obtained by using the so-called bootstrap argument; see
[
36
,
48
]
.

Then we show how the above two points can be obtained.
First, we notice the following:

(i)

the truncated linear operator
\mathcal{L}
generates a continuous semigroup with uniform decay estimates on the stable subspace
V_{\rm{sta}}
defined in (
2.5
);

(ii)

by the definition of cutoff functions
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
, the linear operator
\mathcal{L}^{e}
in (
2.18
)–(
2.19
) agrees exactly with
\mathcal{L}
in
B(0,C_{0})
,

and henceforth carry out the analysis at the level of
the perturbation system (
2.18
)–(
2.19
).
To overcome the difficulties caused by the degeneracy and strong coupling between the velocity and the density
in the spatial dissipation, the main ingredients of our analysis consist of the following:

§
2.3.1
:

establishing spatial decay estimates for
(Q,U)
by the region segmentation method and
some carefully designed weighted energy estimates (see §
5
);

§
2.3.2
:

establishing the lower-order temporal decay estimates of perturbation
(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})
and establishing uniform-in-time bounds for the associated lower-order norms by the region segmentation method and a bootstrap argument (see §
6.1.1
);

§
2.3.3
:

establishing higher-order weighted energy estimates
through some carefully designed weighted energy estimates and the repulsive structure of the self-similar profile (see §
6.1.2
);

§
2.3.4
:

controlling the unstable modes by selecting suitable initial data through a fixed-point argument (see §
6.1.3
).

2.3.1.
Spatial decay estimates

First, based on the properties of
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
,
the weighted energy estimate for perturbation
(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})
is established:

\widetilde{E}_{K}(\tau):=\int\big(|\nabla^{K}\widetilde{Q}(\tau,y)|^{2}+|\nabla^{K}\widetilde{U}(\tau,y)|^{2}\big)\phi^{K}(y)\,\text{d}y\leq 2E.

Second, the spatial decay estimates for
Q
and
(\nabla Q,\nabla\widetilde{Q})
are derived by a region segmentation method.
We choose
R_{0}>0
, depending only on
\nu_{0}
and profile

(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
, such that
|\overline{Q}|+|\overline{U}|\leq C\nu_{0}
in the exterior region
B^{c}(0,R_{0})
.
For any
|y_{0}|\geq R_{0}
, define

\omega_{Q,y_{0}}(\tau):=e^{(\Lambda-1)(\tau-\bar{\tau})}Q(\tau,\,y_{0}e^{(\tau-\bar{\tau})}),

where
y_{0}
and
\bar{\tau}
are chosen such that either
|y_{0}|=R_{0}
or
\bar{\tau}=\tau_{0}
.
Then, by (
2.12
)
1
,
\omega_{Q,y_{0}}(\tau)
satisfies

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\omega_{Q,y_{0}}(\tau)=-e^{(\Lambda-1)(\tau-\bar{\tau})}(U\cdot\nabla Q+\alpha Q\,\mathrm{div}\,U)(\tau,\,y_{0}e^{(\tau-\bar{\tau})}).

(2.26)

Note that
(Q,U)=(\widehat{X}\overline{Q}+\widetilde{Q},\,\widehat{X}\overline{U}+\widetilde{U})
.
By applying the interpolation inequalities, the right-hand side of (
2.26
)
can be controlled by the upper bounds of the cutoff profile
(\widehat{X}\overline{Q},\widehat{X}\overline{U})
and perturbation
(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})
in
B^{c}(0,R_{0})
together with
K
-th order weighted energy estimates for
(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})
.
Integrating (
2.26
) with respect to
\tau
yields the pointwise upper and lower bounds for
Q
along the trajectories
y=y_{0}e^{\tau-\bar{\tau}}
so that,
in the region
B^{c}(0,R_{0})
,

\frac{\nu_{1}}{C}\big\langle\frac{y}{R_{0}}\big\rangle^{-(\Lambda-1)}\leq Q\leq C\nu_{0}\max\Big\{\big\langle\frac{y}{R_{0}}\big\rangle^{-(\Lambda-1)},\,e^{-(\tau-\tau_{0})(\Lambda-1)}\Big\}.

Applying
\nabla
to (
2.12
)
1
and repeating the above argument, we further obtain

|\nabla Q|+|\nabla\widetilde{Q}|\leq C\big\langle\frac{y}{R_{0}}\big\rangle^{-\Lambda},\qquad|y|\geq R_{0}.

In particular, this decay rate is faster than the one directly obtained by
interpolation between
\|Q\|_{L^{\infty}}
and the
K
-th order weighted energy estimates for
(Q,U)
.
In the interior region
B(0,R_{0})
, the bounds follow from interpolation between the upper bounds and the
K
-th order weighted energy estimates for
(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})
, together with the properties of
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
and
\widehat{X}
.

Finally, we turn to the spatial decay of
\nabla U
. In the interior region
B(0,R_{0})
, the derivation of the desired estimates for
\nabla U
is identical to that for
\nabla Q
.
In the exterior region
B^{c}(0,R_{0})
, additional difficulties arise. Applying
\partial_{y_{i}}
to (
2.12
)
2
,
the viscous term takes the form:

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{i}}\mathcal{F}_{\rm{dis}}=C_{\rm{dis}}e^{-\delta_{\rm{dis}}\tau}\Big(

\displaystyle Q^{\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}}L(\partial_{y_{i}}U)+\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}Q^{\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}-1}\partial_{y_{i}}QL(U)+\frac{\delta}{\alpha}Q^{\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}-1}\nabla Q\cdot\mathbb{D}(\partial_{y_{i}}U)\Big.

\displaystyle+\Big.\frac{\delta}{\alpha}Q^{\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}-1}\nabla(\partial_{y_{i}}Q)\cdot\mathbb{D}(U)+\frac{\delta}{\alpha}\big(\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}-1\big)Q^{\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}-2}\,\partial_{y_{i}}Q\,\nabla Q\cdot\mathbb{D}(U)\Big).

Introducing
\omega_{U,y_{0},i}(\tau):=e^{\Lambda(\tau-\bar{\tau})}\partial_{y_{i}}U(\tau,y_{0}e^{\tau-\bar{\tau}})
, we derive

\partial_{\tau}\omega_{U,y_{0},i}=-e^{\Lambda(\tau-\bar{\tau})}\big((\partial_{y_{i}}U\cdot\nabla)U+(U\cdot\nabla)(\partial_{y_{i}}U)+\alpha\partial_{y_{i}}Q\nabla Q+\alpha Q\nabla(\partial_{y_{i}}Q)\big)+\partial_{y_{i}}\mathcal{F}_{\rm{dis}}.

(2.27)

For estimating the right-hand side of (
2.27
), the main difficulties
arise from the viscous terms containing factors of form

Q^{-1}\nabla^{k}Q
, with
k=1,2
.
A direct interpolation between
Q
itself and its
K
-th order weighted energy does not provide a sufficiently fast decay rate. However, we have already shown that
|\nabla Q|
exhibits faster spatial decay than
Q
itself.
Then, according to the Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequality (
A.9
), we obtain the pointwise bound

|Q^{-1}\nabla^{k}Q|\leq C(\nu_{0})\big\langle\frac{y}{R_{0}}\big\rangle^{-1+\eta-\Lambda}\qquad\text{for}\ \ k=1,2.

All remaining terms in (
2.27
) can be handled
by the lower bound of
Q
, as well as the upper bounds and the
K
-th order weighted energy for
(Q,U)
.
Combining all the above bounds, we obtain

|\nabla U|+|\nabla\widetilde{U}|\leq C\big\langle\frac{y}{R_{0}}\big\rangle^{-\Lambda}\qquad\text{for all $y\in B^{c}(0,R_{0})$.}

2.3.2.
Lower-order temporal decay estimates

First, the spatial decay estimates for
(Q,U)
established above, together with the properties of the cutoff function
\widehat{X}
,
yield the temporal decay bounds for terms

\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{N}
, and

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{dis}}
.
These bounds provide the basic preparatory estimates for the temporal decay
analysis of the perturbation
(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})
itself and its fourth-order spatial derivatives.

Next, we treat the interior and exterior regions separately. Recall that

V_{\mathrm{sta}}
and
V_{\mathrm{uns}}
are invariant subspaces of

\mathcal{L}
.
On the stable space
V_{\mathrm{sta}}
,
\mathcal{L}
is dissipative.
On the unstable space
V_{\mathrm{uns}}
, which is finite-dimensional with

\dim V_{\mathrm{uns}}=N
, we assume that coefficients
\{\hat{k}_{i}\}
are chosen so that the evolution of the unstable initial component

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\hat{k}_{i}(\varphi_{i,\zeta},\varphi_{i,u})
remains controlled:

\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{N}k_{i}(\tau)(\varphi_{i,\zeta},\varphi_{i,u})\Big\|_{X}\leq\nu_{1}e^{-\varepsilon(\tau-\tau_{0})}\qquad\text{for all}\,\,\,\tau\geq\tau_{0}.

It is observed that the linear operator
\mathcal{L}^{e}
appearing in
(
2.18
)–(
2.19
) agrees with
\mathcal{L}
in
B(0,C_{0})
. As a consequence, the above properties of dissipativity and boundedness
remain valid for
\mathcal{L}^{e}
when restricted to
B(0,C_{0})
. This allows us to identify the interior region as
B(0,C_{0})
.
Combining these properties with the decay estimates obtained for

\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{N}
, and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{dis}}
,
it follows that, for
0\leq j\leq 4
,

\|\nabla^{j}\widetilde{Q}\|_{L^{\infty}(B(0,C_{0}))}+\|\nabla^{j}\widetilde{U}\|_{L^{\infty}(B(0,C_{0}))}\leq C\frac{\nu_{1}}{\nu_{g}}e^{-\varepsilon(\tau-\tau_{0})}.

On the other hand, in the exterior region
B^{c}(0,C_{0})
, we rewrite the perturbed system (
2.18
)–(
2.19
)
along the trajectories:
y=y_{0}e^{\tau-\bar{\tau}}
. Following the same strategy as the one used in the derivation of the spatial decay estimates for
Q
in §
2.3.1
, and combining the Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequality with the bounds for
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{N}
, and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{dis}}
obtained previously, we can obtain
the corresponding temporal decay bounds in the exterior region
B^{c}(0,C_{0})
:

\max\{|\widetilde{Q}|,\ |\widetilde{U}|\}\leq\frac{{\nu_{0}}}{200}e^{-\varepsilon(\tau-\tau_{0})}\qquad\text{for all}\,\,\,\tau\geq\tau_{0}.

We now turn to the temporal decay estimates for the fourth-order spatial derivatives of

(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})
. The main difficulty arises from the
derivatives of the nonlinear terms
\mathcal{N}
and the dissipative term

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{dis}}
. For
\mathcal{N}
, a Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequality is employed to obtain the required bounds.
For
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{dis}}
, the spatial decay estimates established earlier ensure the
integrability with respect to
y
, while the factor

e^{-\delta_{\mathrm{dis}}\tau}
provides the necessary temporal decay. As a consequence, we conclude

\max\big\{\|\nabla^{4}\widetilde{Q}\|_{L^{2}(B^{c}(0,C_{0}))},\,\|\nabla^{4}\widetilde{U}\|_{L^{2}(B^{c}(0,C_{0}))}\big\}\leq\frac{\nu_{0}}{2}e^{-\varepsilon(\tau-\tau_{0})}\qquad\text{for all}\,\,\,\tau\geq\tau_{0}.

2.3.3.
K
-th order weighted estimates

Now we give the
K
-th order weighted energy estimate:

E_{K}(\tau)=\int\big(|\nabla^{K}Q(\tau,y)|^{2}+|\nabla^{K}U(\tau,y)|^{2}\big)\phi^{K}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\leq E,

where the weight function
\phi\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is defined in (
2.7
).
Applying
\partial_{\beta}
(
\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\cdots,\beta_{K})
with
|\beta|=K
) to
(
2.12
)
and denoting
W_{\beta}:=(\partial_{\beta}Q,\partial_{\beta}U)^{\top}
yield

(\partial_{\tau}+\Lambda-1+K)W_{\beta}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}A_{j}(Q,U;y)\,\partial_{y_{j}}W_{\beta}=\mathcal{C}_{\beta}+\binom{0}{\partial_{\beta}\mathcal{F}_{\rm dis}},

(2.28)

where
A_{j}(Q,U;y),j=1,2,3
, are symmetric matrices,
and
\mathcal{C}_{\beta}
collects the lower-order terms:

\mathcal{C}_{\beta}:=-\binom{[\partial_{\beta},\,U\cdot\nabla]Q}{[\partial_{\beta},\,U\cdot\nabla]U}-\alpha\binom{[\partial_{\beta},\,Q]\,\mathrm{div}U}{[\partial_{\beta},\,Q]\nabla Q}.

By the symmetric structure of (
2.28
), the construction of the weight function
\phi
,
and the repulsivity of the radial and angular
components of profile
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
, the convective terms can be controlled by the weighted energy
E_{K}
.
More precisely, the required repulsivity is guaranteed by the conditions:

\displaystyle-\partial_{r}(\widehat{X}\overline{\mathcal{U}})+\alpha|\partial_{r}(\widehat{X}\overline{Q})|+\frac{\nabla\phi\cdot(y+\widehat{X}\overline{U})+|\nabla\phi|\widehat{X}\overline{Q}}{2\phi}

\displaystyle\leq 1-\frac{\min\{\tilde{\eta},\eta\}}{2},

\displaystyle-\frac{\widehat{X}\overline{\mathcal{U}}}{r}+\alpha|\partial_{r}(\widehat{X}\overline{Q})|+\frac{\nabla\phi\cdot(y+\widehat{X}\overline{U})+|\nabla\phi|\widehat{X}\overline{Q}}{2\phi}

\displaystyle\leq 1-\frac{\min\{\tilde{\eta},\eta\}}{2},

where
r=|y|
, and
0<\tilde{\eta},\eta<1
are two small constants arising from the repulsivity properties (
B.6
)–(
B.7
) and the definition of the weight function in (
2.7
), respectively.
Moreover, the terms
\mathcal{C}_{\beta}
and
\partial_{\beta}\mathcal{F}_{\rm{dis}}
can be handled by direct calculations or integration by parts.

As a result, the weighted energy
E_{K}(\tau)
satisfies the differential inequality:

\Big(\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}+\Lambda-1+K\Big)E_{K}\;\leq\;K\Big(1-\frac{\min\{\tilde{\eta},\eta\}}{2}\Big)E_{K}+CE+e^{-\delta_{\rm dis}(\tau-\tau_{0})},

which, along with the Grönwall inequality, yields the desired bound for
E_{K}(\tau)
.

2.3.4.
Control of the unstable modes of
(\overset{\approx}{Q},\overset{\approx}{U})
by the selection of initial data.

Since the linear operator
\mathcal{L}
generates a continuous semigroup on space
X
consisting of functions supported in region
B(0,3C_{0})
,
we consider the truncated equations (
4.14
) with the initial data
(\overset{\approx}{Q}_{0},\overset{\approx}{U}_{0})
. Under the decomposition
X=V_{\mathrm{sta}}\oplus V_{\mathrm{uns}}
, the initial data for (
4.14
) can be decomposed
into a stable component and an unstable one:

\overset{\approx}{Q}_{0}=\chi_{2}\widetilde{Q}_{0}^{*}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\hat{k}_{i}\varphi_{i,\zeta},\qquad\overset{\approx}{U}_{0}=\chi_{2}\widetilde{U}_{0}^{*}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\hat{k}_{i}\varphi_{i,u},

(2.29)

where
\{(\varphi_{i,\zeta},\varphi_{i,u})\}_{i=1}^{N}
is a normalized basis of the unstable subspace
V_{\mathrm{uns}}
constructed in Lemma
C.3
in Appendix
C
and coefficients
\{\hat{k}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
are to be determined.
Since the stable component of
(\overset{\approx}{Q},\overset{\approx}{U})
has already been controlled as above, it remains to analyze the unstable modes.
We therefore focus on the unstable modes associated with operator
\mathcal{L}
.
Denote

k(\tau)=P_{\rm{uns}}(\overset{\approx}{Q}(\cdot,\tau),\overset{\approx}{U}(\cdot,\tau))=\sum_{i=1}^{N}k_{i}(\tau)(\varphi_{i,\zeta},\varphi_{i,u}).

Since
V_{\mathrm{uns}}
is invariant with respect to the linear operator
\mathcal{L}
, the unstable coefficients satisfy

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}k(\tau)=\mathcal{L}k(\tau)+P_{\rm{uns}}(\chi_{2}\mathcal{F}),\qquad\displaystyle k(\tau_{0})=\big(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\hat{k}_{i}\varphi_{i,\zeta},\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}\hat{k}_{i}\varphi_{i,u}\big),

(2.30)

where
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{N}+\mathcal{E}+\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{dis}}
.
This differential equation is generally unstable, and solutions with arbitrary initial data may grow exponentially.
For the metric,
\Upsilon
, determined in Lemma
C.3
, let

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(\tau):=\big\{k(\tau)\in V_{\mathrm{uns}}:\,\|k(\tau)\|_{\Upsilon}\leq\nu_{1}^{\frac{11}{10}}e^{-\frac{4}{3}\varepsilon(\tau-\tau_{0})}\big\}.

By the spectral properties of
\mathcal{L}
restricted to
V_{\mathrm{uns}}
,
Lemma
6.8
shows that any trajectory reaching the boundary of
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(\tau)
immediately exits the region.
This outgoing property provides a topological selection argument based on Brouwer’s fixed point theorem, which yields at least one choice of initial unstable coefficients
\{\hat{k}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
for which the corresponding solution remains in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(\tau)
for all
\tau\geq\tau_{0}
.

3.
Local-In-Time Well-Posedness of the Three-Dimensional Smooth Solutions

This section is devoted to establishing the desired local-in-time well-posedness of smooth solutions of the Cauchy problem (
2.12
)–(
2.16
). In particular, we provide a higher-order weighted regularity estimate for the corresponding solutions, which plays a crucial role in initiating the bootstrap argument and in controlling the degenerate dissipative structure introduced by the reformulation. Moreover, throughout §
3
–§
9
, we always assume that
C\geq 1
denotes a generic constant depending only on
(\Lambda,\gamma,\delta,a_{1},a_{2})
, and
C(\nu_{1},\cdots\!,\nu_{k})\geq 1
denotes a generic constant depending on
C

and parameters
\nu_{1},\cdots\!,\nu_{k}
, which may be different at each occurrence.

Our main result in this section can be stated as follows:

Theorem 3.1
.

Assume that parameters
(\Lambda,\alpha,\delta,a_{1},a_{2})
satisfy

\Lambda>1,\quad\alpha>0,\quad 0<\delta<1,\quad a_{1}>0,\quad 2a_{1}+3a_{2}\geq 0,

(3.1)

and
K\geq 4
is an integer.
Let the initial data
(Q_{0},U_{0})
satisfy

\inf_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}Q_{0}(y)>0,\quad Q_{0}-Q^{*}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad U_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),

(3.2)

for some integer
s\geq K
.
Then there exists a time
\tau_{*}>\tau_{0}
such that the Cauchy
problem (
2.12
)
–
(
2.16
) admits
a unique smooth solution
(Q,U)(\tau,y)
in
[\tau_{0},\tau_{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}

satisfying

\begin{split}&\inf_{(\tau,y)\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}Q(\tau,y)>0,\ \ Q-Q^{*}\in C([\tau_{0},\tau_{*}];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),\\
&\partial_{\tau}Q\in C([\tau_{0},\tau_{*}];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),\ \ U\in C([\tau_{0},\tau_{*}];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))\cap L^{2}([\tau_{0},\tau_{*}];H^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),\\[2.0pt]
&\partial_{\tau}U\in C([\tau_{0},\tau_{*}];H^{s-2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))\cap L^{2}([\tau_{0},\tau_{*}];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})).\end{split}

(3.3)

Moreover, if
E_{K}(\tau_{0})<\infty
,

\text{\rm ess}\!\!\!\sup_{\tau_{0}\leq\tau\leq\tau_{*}}E_{K}(\tau)<\infty.

(3.4)

3.1.
The Local Well-Posedness of Smooth Solutions of the Cauchy Problem (
2.8
)

In order to study the local-in-time well-posedness of smooth solutions of the Cauchy problem (
2.12
)–(
2.16
), we first consider the Cauchy problem (
2.8
).

Theorem 3.2
.

Assume that the physical parameters
(\gamma,\delta,a_{1},a_{2})
satisfy

\gamma>1,\quad 0<\delta<1,\quad a_{1}>0,\quad 2a_{1}+3a_{2}\geq 0,

(3.5)

and
K\geq 4
is an integer.
If the initial data
(c_{0},u_{0})
satisfy

\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}c_{0}(x)>0,\quad c_{0}-\bar{c}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),

(3.6)

for some integer
s\geq K
and constant
\bar{c}>0
, then there exists

T_{*}>0
such that the Cauchy problem (
2.8
) admits a unique smooth solution
(c,u)(t,x)
in
[0,T_{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfying

\begin{split}&\inf_{(t,x)\in[0,T_{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}c(t,x)>0,\ \ c-\bar{c}\in C([0,T_{*}];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),\\
&\partial_{t}c\in C([0,T_{*}];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),\ \ u\in C([0,T_{*}];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))\cap L^{2}([0,T_{*}];H^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),\\[2.0pt]
&\partial_{t}u\in C([0,T_{*}];H^{s-2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))\cap L^{2}([0,T_{*}];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})).\end{split}

(3.7)

Since one can obtain the positive lower bound of
c
via its transport equation
\eqref{eq:1.2a}_{1}
in some finite time,
\eqref{eq:1.2a}_{2}
becomes a parabolic system for
u
with the source term
c^{\frac{\delta-1-\alpha}{\alpha}}\nabla_{x}c\cdot\mathbb{D}_{x}(u)
.
Then, by adapting the classical method in Matsumura-Nishida
[
36
]
and exploiting the equations in (
2.8
),
one can establish the desired local well-posedness of smooth solutions when
\bar{c}>0
. For brevity, we omit the details of proof.

3.2.
The Local-In-Time Well-Posedness of Smooth Solutions of the Cauchy
Problem (
2.12
)–(
2.16
)

Now we are ready to give the proof for Theorem
3.1
.

Proof.

We divide the proof into three steps.
In what follows, we focus on the case
s=K
, since the other cases
follow by analogous arguments.

1.
By the assumption of Theorem 3.1, the initial data
(Q_{0},U_{0})
satisfy

\inf_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}Q_{0}(y)>0,\qquad Q_{0}-Q^{*}\in H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\qquad U_{0}\in H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),

(3.8)

where
Q^{*}=Q^{*}(\tau_{0})
is given by (
2.16
).

It follows from (
2.15
) that

c_{0}(x)=\Lambda^{-1}e^{(\Lambda-1)\tau_{0}}Q_{0}(e^{\tau_{0}}x),\qquad u_{0}(x)=\Lambda^{-1}e^{(\Lambda-1)\tau_{0}}U_{0}(e^{\tau_{0}}x).

(3.9)

Based on the relation:
x=e^{-\tau_{0}}y
, we obtain

\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}c_{0}(x)=\inf_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}c_{0}(e^{-\tau_{0}}y)=\Lambda^{-1}e^{(\Lambda-1)\tau_{0}}\inf_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}Q_{0}(y)>0.

Let
\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\cdots,\beta_{k})
be a multi-index with

\beta_{i}\in\{1,2,3\}
for
i=1,\cdots,k
, where
k\in[0,K]
is an integer. By the chain rule and the fact that

\partial_{x_{i}}y_{j}=e^{\tau_{0}}\delta_{ij}
, one has

\partial^{x}_{\beta}u_{0}(x)=\Lambda^{-1}e^{(\Lambda-1)\tau_{0}}e^{|\beta|\tau_{0}}\,\,\partial_{\beta}^{y}U_{0}(y).

Since
\text{d}x=e^{-3\tau_{0}}\,\text{d}y
, it follows that

\displaystyle\|\partial^{x}_{\beta}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{-2}e^{2(\Lambda-1)\tau_{0}}e^{2|\beta|\tau_{0}}\int|\partial^{y}_{\beta}U_{0}(y)|^{2}\,e^{-3\tau_{0}}\text{d}y

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{-2}e^{2(\Lambda-1)\tau_{0}}e^{(2|\beta|-3)\tau_{0}}\,\|\partial^{y}_{\beta}U_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{y}}^{2}.

Summing over
|\beta|\leq K
yields

\|u_{0}\|_{H^{K}_{x}}\leq C(K,\tau_{0})\,\|U_{0}\|_{H^{K}_{y}},

where

C(K,\tau_{0})=\Lambda^{-1}e^{(\Lambda-1)\tau_{0}}\Big(\sum_{|\beta|\leq K}e^{\frac{2|\beta|-3}{2}\tau_{0}}\Big).

Thus,
u_{0}\in H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Similarly, we can also show that
c_{0}-\bar{c}\in H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

2.

According to Step 1 and Theorem
3.2
, there exist both
T_{*}>0
and
a unique smooth solution
(c,u)
in
[0,T_{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
of (
2.8
).
Now we introduce the self-similar variables for some
T>0
:

\tau=-\frac{\log(T-t)}{\Lambda},\qquad y=\frac{x}{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}}=e^{\tau}x.

Denote
\tau_{*}=-\frac{\log(T-T_{*})}{\Lambda}
.
According to (
2.11
), the vector function
(Q,U)(\tau,y)
is well-defined
in
[\tau_{0},\tau_{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
It is direct to check that
(Q,U)

satisfies the Cauchy problem (
2.12
)–(
2.16
) pointwise.
Next, we need to show that (
3.3
)–(
3.4
) hold.

Let
\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\cdots,\beta_{k})
be a multi-index with

\beta_{i}\in\{1,2,3\}
for
i=1,\cdots,k
, where
k\in[0,K]
is an integer.
By the chain rule and the identity
\partial_{y_{j}}x_{i}=e^{-\tau}\delta_{ij}
,
one has

\partial^{y}_{\beta}U(\tau,y)=\Lambda e^{-(\Lambda-1)\tau}e^{-|\beta|\tau}(\partial^{x}_{\beta}u)(t,x),\qquad x=e^{-\tau}y.

Since
\text{d}y=e^{3\tau}\,\text{d}x
, it follows that

\displaystyle\|\partial^{y}_{\beta}U(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{y}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{2}e^{-2(\Lambda-1)\tau}e^{-2|\beta|\tau}\int|\partial^{x}_{\beta}u(t,x)|^{2}e^{3\tau}\,\text{d}x

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{2}e^{-2(\Lambda-1)\tau}e^{(3-2|\beta|)\tau}\|\partial^{x}_{\beta}u(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}.

Summing over all
|\beta|\leq K
, we obtain

\|U(\tau,\cdot)\|_{H^{K}_{y}}\leq C(K,\tau)\,\|u(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{K}_{x}},\qquad t=T-e^{-\Lambda\tau},

where

C(K,\tau)=\Lambda e^{-(\Lambda-1)\tau}\Big(\sum_{|\beta|\leq K}e^{\frac{3-2|\beta|}{2}\tau}\Big)

is bounded on the finite interval
[\tau_{0},\tau_{*}]
.

Define the scaling operator
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}f(y):=f(e^{-\tau}y)
. Then

\|\mathcal{S}_{\tau}f\|_{H^{K}}\leq\Big(\sum_{|\beta|\leq K}e^{\frac{3-2|\beta|}{2}\tau}\Big)\|f\|_{H^{K}},\qquad\,\,\text{ for all $f\in H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3})$ and $\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{*}]$}.

Moreover, the family,
\{\mathcal{S}_{\tau}\}_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}}
, satisfies the group property:

\mathcal{S}_{\tau_{2}}f(y)=f(e^{-\tau_{2}}y)=f(e^{-\tau_{1}-(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})}y)=\mathcal{S}_{\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}}(\mathcal{S}_{\tau_{1}}f(y)),\qquad\mathcal{S}_{0}f(y)=f(y),

for all
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.

Indeed, for
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, the map:
\tau\mapsto\mathcal{S}_{\tau}f

is continuous in
H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
by dominated convergence.
Since
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is dense in
H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
is locally bounded,
the strong continuity extends to all
f\in H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.
Consequently,
\{\mathcal{S}_{\tau}\}_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}}
forms a strongly continuous one-parameter group on
H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3}).

Therefore, for
\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{*}]

U(\tau,\cdot)=\Lambda e^{-(\Lambda-1)\tau}\mathcal{S}_{\tau}(u(t(\tau),\cdot))\qquad\mbox{with $t(\tau):=T-e^{-\Lambda\tau}$}.

Since the map:
t\mapsto u(t,\cdot)
is continuous in
H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
the change of variables in time
\tau\mapsto t(\tau)
is continuous,
and
\{\mathcal{S}_{\tau}\}_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}}
is a strongly continuous group on
H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
we conclude

U\in C([\tau_{0},\tau_{*}];H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3})).

Moreover, it follows from the relation:
\text{d}t=\Lambda(T-t)\,\text{d}\tau
,
(
2.11
), and

u\in L^{2}([0,T_{*}];H^{K+1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
that

U\in L^{2}([\tau_{0},\tau_{*}];H^{K+1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})).

The analogous arguments can be applied to
Q
and the corresponding time derivatives to
further obtain

\displaystyle\inf_{(\tau,y)\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}Q(\tau,y)>0,\quad Q-Q^{*}\in C([\tau_{0},\tau_{*}];H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),

(3.10)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}Q\in C([\tau_{0},\tau_{*}];H^{K-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),\quad\partial_{\tau}U\in C([\tau_{0},\tau_{*}];H^{K-2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))\cap L^{2}([\tau_{0},\tau_{*}];H^{K-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})).

3.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}
be a non-increasing
C^{2}
function satisfying

\displaystyle f(s)=\begin{cases}1&s\in[0,\frac{1}{2}],\\[2.0pt]
\text{non-negative polynomial}&s\in[\frac{1}{2},1],\\[2.0pt]
e^{-s}&s\geq 1.\end{cases}

For any
R>1
, we define
f_{R}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
by
f_{R}(y)=f(\frac{|y|}{R})
.
Clearly, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle|f^{\prime}(s)|\leq Cf(s).

Then, for any
p\geq 0
,

\displaystyle|y|^{p}f_{R}(y)\leq C,\qquad\lim_{|y|\to\infty}|y|^{p}f_{R}(y)=0.

(3.11)

Let
\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\cdots,\beta_{K})
be a multi-index with

\beta_{i}\in\{1,2,3\}
for
i=1,\cdots,K
. Applying
\partial_{\beta}
to (
2.12
)
and using the identity:
\partial_{\beta}((y\cdot\nabla)f)=K\partial_{\beta}f+(y\cdot\nabla)\partial_{\beta}f
,
we have

\displaystyle(\partial_{\tau}+\Lambda-1+K)\partial_{\beta}Q+(y\cdot\nabla)\partial_{\beta}Q+\partial_{\beta}(U\cdot\nabla Q)+\alpha\partial_{\beta}(Q\,\mathrm{div}U)=0,

(3.12)

\displaystyle(\partial_{\tau}+\Lambda-1+K)\partial_{\beta}U+(y\cdot\nabla)\partial_{\beta}U+\partial_{\beta}(U\cdot\nabla U)+\alpha\partial_{\beta}(Q\nabla Q)=\partial_{\beta}\mathcal{F}_{\rm dis},

where

\mathcal{F}_{\rm dis}=C_{\rm{dis}}e^{-\delta_{\rm{dis}}\tau}\big(Q^{\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}}L(U)+\frac{\delta}{\alpha}Q^{\frac{\delta-1-\alpha}{\alpha}}\nabla Q\cdot\mathbb{D}(U)\big).

Now, we define a new energy

E_{K,R}(\tau)=\int\big(|\nabla^{K}Q|^{2}+|\nabla^{K}U|^{2}\big)\phi^{K}f_{R}\,\mathrm{d}y=\sum_{|\beta|=K}\int\big(|\partial_{\beta}Q|^{2}+|\partial_{\beta}U|^{2}\big)\phi^{K}f_{R}\,\mathrm{d}y,

(3.13)

where
\partial_{\beta}U=(\partial_{\beta}U_{1},\partial_{\beta}U_{2},\partial_{\beta}U_{3})^{\top}
, and the weight function
\phi\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is defined in (
2.7
).

Multiplying each equation of (
3.12
) by
