4.1
.

According to Theorem
3.1
, there exists a unique smooth solution
(Q,U)
of the Cauchy problem (
2.12
)–(
2.16
) in
[\tau_{0},\tau_{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. To extend this solution to be a global-in-time one, we define the set

\mathscr{S}=\{\tau>\tau_{0}:\text{the solution exists on }[\tau_{0},\tau]\text{ and satisfies }\eqref{lowerestimate1}\text{ and }\eqref{higherestimate3}\}.

(7.1)

Let
\bar{\tau}_{*}=\sup\mathscr{S}
. We aim to show that
\bar{\tau}_{*}=\infty
by contradiction.

Let
\hat{\tau}
be any fixed time satisfying
\hat{\tau}\in(\tau_{0},\bar{\tau}_{*})
. Collecting the uniform
a priori
bounds obtained in Proposition
4.1
and Lemma
6.9
yields that, for any
\tau\in[\tau_{0},\hat{\tau}]
,

\displaystyle\|(Q-Q^{*})(\tau,\cdot)\|^{2}_{H^{K}}+\|U(\tau,\cdot)\|^{2}_{H^{K}}+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau}\|U(\upsilon,\cdot)\|^{2}_{H^{K+1}}\text{d}\upsilon

\displaystyle\leq C(\hat{\tau}),

(7.2)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\tau}Q(\tau,\cdot)\|^{2}_{H^{K-1}}+\|\partial_{\tau}U(\tau,\cdot)\|^{2}_{H^{K-2}}+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau}\|\partial_{\tau}U(\upsilon,\cdot)\|^{2}_{H^{K-1}}\text{d}\upsilon

\displaystyle\leq C(\hat{\tau}),

\displaystyle\|P_{\rm{uns}}(\overset{\approx}{Q},\overset{\approx}{U})(\tau,\cdot)\|_{X}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu_{1}}{2},

\displaystyle\max\big\{\|\widetilde{Q}(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}},\ \|\widetilde{U}(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}}\big\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu_{0}}{200},

\displaystyle\max\big\{\|\nabla^{4}\widetilde{Q}(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(B^{c}(0,C_{0}))},\ \|\nabla^{4}\widetilde{U}(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(B^{c}(0,C_{0}))}\big\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu_{0}}{2},

\displaystyle E_{K}(\tau)=\int\big(|\nabla^{K}Q(\tau,y)|^{2}+|\nabla^{K}U(\tau,y)|^{2}\big)\phi^{K}(y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\frac{E}{2}.

Clearly,
\bar{\tau}_{*}\geq\tau_{*}
. If
\bar{\tau}_{*}<\infty
, according to the uniform
a priori
estimates (
7.2
) and the standard weak convergence arguments, for any sequence
\{\tau_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
satisfying

0<\tau_{k}<\bar{\tau}_{*},\qquad\,\,\tau_{k}\to\bar{\tau}_{*}\,\,\,\,\,\text{as $k\to\infty$},

there exists a subsequence (still denoted by)
\{\tau_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
and limits
(Q,U)(\bar{\tau}_{*},y)
satisfying

Q(\bar{\tau}_{*},y)-Q^{*}\in H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad U(\bar{\tau}_{*},y)\in H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),

and, by Lemma
A.2
, as
k\to\infty
,

\displaystyle Q(\tau_{k},y)-Q^{*}\to Q(\bar{\tau}_{*},y)-Q^{*}

\displaystyle\text{weakly \ in}\ H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),

(7.3)

\displaystyle U(\tau_{k},y)\to U(\bar{\tau}_{*},y)

\displaystyle\text{weakly \ in}\ H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),

\displaystyle P_{\mathrm{uns}}(\overset{\approx}{Q},\overset{\approx}{U})(\tau_{k},y)\to P_{\mathrm{uns}}(\overset{\approx}{Q},\overset{\approx}{U})(\bar{\tau}_{*},y)

\displaystyle\text{weakly \ in}\ H_{0}^{m}(B(0,3C_{0})),

\displaystyle(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})(\tau_{k},y)\to(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})(\bar{\tau}_{*},y)

\displaystyle\text{weakly${}^{*}$ \ in}\ L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),

\displaystyle(\nabla^{4}\widetilde{Q},\nabla^{4}\widetilde{U})(\tau_{k},y)\to(\nabla^{4}\widetilde{Q},\nabla^{4}\widetilde{U})(\bar{\tau}_{*},y)

\displaystyle\text{weakly \ in}\ L^{2}(B^{c}(0,C_{0})),

\displaystyle(\nabla^{K}Q\phi^{\frac{K}{2}},\nabla^{K}U\phi^{\frac{K}{2}})(\tau_{k},y)\to(\nabla^{K}Q\phi^{\frac{K}{2}},\nabla^{K}U\phi^{\frac{K}{2}})(\bar{\tau}_{*},y)

\displaystyle\text{weakly \ in}\ L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}).

It follows from (
7.2
) and the lower semi-continuity of norms that

\displaystyle\|(Q-Q^{*})(\bar{\tau}_{*},\cdot)\|_{H^{K}}^{2}+\|U(\bar{\tau}_{*},\cdot)\|_{H^{K}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(\bar{\tau}_{*}),

(7.4)

\displaystyle\|P_{\rm{uns}}(\overset{\approx}{Q},\overset{\approx}{U})(\bar{\tau}_{*},\cdot)\|_{X}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu_{1}}{2},

\displaystyle\max\big\{\|\widetilde{Q}(\bar{\tau}_{*},\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}},\ \|\widetilde{U}(\bar{\tau}_{*},\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}}\big\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu_{0}}{200},

\displaystyle\max\big\{\|\nabla^{4}\widetilde{Q}(\bar{\tau}_{*},\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(B^{c}(0,C_{0}))},\ \|\nabla^{4}\widetilde{U}(\bar{\tau}_{*},\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(B^{c}(0,C_{0}))}\big\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu_{0}}{2},

\displaystyle E_{K}(\bar{\tau}_{*})=\int\big(|\nabla^{K}Q(\bar{\tau}_{*},y)|^{2}+|\nabla^{K}U(\bar{\tau}_{*},y)|^{2}\big)\phi^{K}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\frac{E}{2}.

We take the limit
(Q,U)(\bar{\tau}_{*},y)
as new initial data at
\tau=\bar{\tau}_{*}
.
Theorem
3.1
ensures the existence of a positive time
\iota_{*}>0

such that the smooth solution exists on
[\bar{\tau}_{*},\bar{\tau}_{*}+\iota_{*}]
.

(Q,U)(\bar{\tau}_{*},y)
satisfies (
4.12
)–(
4.13
), and the bounds in (
7.2
) yield

(Q-Q^{*},U)\in C([\bar{\tau}_{*},\bar{\tau}_{*}+\iota_{*}];H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3})).

It follows from (
3.35
)–(
3.36
) and (
7.4
)
5
that, for all
\tau\in[\bar{\tau}_{*},\bar{\tau}_{*}+\iota_{*}]
,

\displaystyle E_{K}(\tau)

\displaystyle\leq e^{C(K,\nu_{1},\iota_{*})(\tau-\bar{\tau}_{*})}E_{K}(\bar{\tau}_{*})+e^{C(K,\nu_{1},\iota_{*})(\tau-\bar{\tau}_{*})}-1

(7.5)

\displaystyle\leq e^{C(K,\nu_{1},\iota_{*})(\tau-\bar{\tau}_{*})}\frac{E}{2}+e^{C(K,\nu_{1},\iota_{*})(\tau-\bar{\tau}_{*})}-1.

By the continuity of factor
e^{C(K,\nu_{1},\iota_{*})(\tau-\bar{\tau}_{*})}
, there exists
\iota_{1}\in(0,\iota_{*}]

such that estimate (
4.21
) remains valid on the extended interval
[\bar{\tau}_{*},\bar{\tau}_{*}+\iota_{1}]
.

On the other hand, since

(Q-Q^{*},\,U)\in C([\bar{\tau}_{*},\bar{\tau}_{*}+\iota_{*}];H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))

and
K\geq 4
, the Sobolev embedding and the bounds of
(\widehat{X}\overline{Q},\,\widehat{X}\overline{U})

imply

(\widetilde{Q},\,\widetilde{U})\in C([\bar{\tau}_{*},\bar{\tau}_{*}+\iota_{*}];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})).

Together with (
7.4
)
3
, this yields that there exists
\iota_{2}\in(0,\iota_{*}]
such that
estimate (
4.19
) remains valid on the extended interval
[\bar{\tau}_{*},\bar{\tau}_{*}+\iota_{2}]
.
Consequently, there exists
\iota=\min\{\iota_{1},\iota_{2}\}
such that estimates
(
4.19
)
and
(
4.21
)

are both valid on the extended interval
[\bar{\tau}_{*},\bar{\tau}_{*}+\iota]
.

This contradicts the definition of
\bar{\tau}_{*}
as the supremum of
\mathscr{S}
. Therefore, we conclude that
\bar{\tau}_{*}=\infty
, yielding the global-in-time existence of the solution. This completes the proof of Theorem
2.1
.

8.
Development of Implosions of Solutions

Based on the global-in-time stability result obtained in Theorem
2.1
for the reformulated system (
2.12
) in self-similar coordinates,
we are now ready to consider the corresponding development of implosions of solutions to the degenerate
CNS
(
1.1
) in Eulerian coordinates.

We divide the proof into two steps.

1. Condition
(P_{1})
. Under condition
(P_{1})
in Theorem
1.1
, for any fixed
\gamma\in(1,1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}})
, there exists
\Lambda\in(1,\Lambda^{*}(\gamma))
introduced in Lemma
B.1
such that the steady Euler system (
1.16
) admits a smooth, spherically symmetric solution
(\overline{Q},\,\overline{U})
. Furthermore,
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
satisfies
\delta_{\rm dis}>0
.
Then all the assumptions for parameters
(\gamma,\Lambda,\delta)
required in Theorem
2.1
are satisfied.

For any vector function
(\widetilde{Q}^{*}_{0},\widetilde{U}^{*}_{0})
satisfying conditions (
4.10
)–(
4.13
), we can take
\{\hat{k}_{i}\}
as in Proposition
6.1
. Then the initial data function for the Cauchy problem (
2.12
)–(
2.16
) is given by

Q_{0}=\widehat{X}\overline{Q}+\widetilde{Q}_{0}^{*}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\hat{k}_{i}\varphi_{i,\zeta},\qquad U_{0}=\widehat{X}\overline{U}+\widetilde{U}_{0}^{*}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\hat{k}_{i}\varphi_{i,u}.

According to the self-similar scaling (
2.10
) and Theorem
2.1
,
we see that, for all
\tau\geq\tau_{0}
,

\max\big\{\|\widetilde{Q}(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}},\ \|\widetilde{U}(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}}\big\}\leq\frac{\nu_{0}}{100}e^{-\varepsilon(\tau-\tau_{0})}\leq\frac{\nu_{0}}{100}\left(\frac{T-t}{T}\right)^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\Lambda}}.

It follows from (
2.10
)–(
2.11
) and
\widehat{X}(y,\tau)=\mathscr{X}(e^{-\tau}y)
that

\displaystyle c(t,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Lambda(T-t)^{1-\frac{1}{\Lambda}}}\Big(\mathscr{X}(x)\overline{Q}(\frac{x}{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}})+\widetilde{Q}(-\frac{\log(T-t)}{\Lambda},\frac{x}{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}})\Big),

\displaystyle u(t,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Lambda(T-t)^{1-\frac{1}{\Lambda}}}\Big(\mathscr{X}(x)\overline{U}(\frac{x}{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}})+\widetilde{U}(-\frac{\log(T-t)}{\Lambda},\frac{x}{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}})\Big).

Moreover, recalling that
c=\frac{1}{\alpha}\rho^{\alpha}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to T^{-}}(\alpha^{-1}\Lambda(T-t)^{1-\frac{1}{\Lambda}})^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\rho(t,x)

\displaystyle=\mathscr{X}^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}(x)\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\overline{Q}^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}(\frac{x}{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}}),

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\Lambda(T-t)^{1-\frac{1}{\Lambda}}u(t,x)

\displaystyle=\mathscr{X}(x)\lim_{t\to T^{-}}\overline{U}(\frac{x}{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}}).

In view of the properties of the self-similar profile
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
,
these asymptotics show that, as
t\to T^{-}
, density
\rho(t,x)
blows up at the origin,
while velocity
u(t,x)
becomes unbounded in any arbitrarily small neighborhood of the origin.
Consequently, the solution to
CNS
develops an implosion-type singularity at time
T
.

2. Condition
(P_{2})
. Now we consider the analysis under condition
(P_{2})
.
For any fixed
\gamma\in(1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}})\setminus\mathcal{J}
,
where the set
\mathcal{J}
(possibly empty) introduced in Lemma
B.1
,
there exists a discrete sequence of scaling parameters
\{\Lambda_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
satisfying

1<\Lambda_{n}<\Lambda^{*}(\gamma),\qquad\lim_{n\to\infty}\Lambda_{n}=\Lambda^{*}(\gamma),

such that the steady Euler system (
1.16
) with
\Lambda_{n}
(
n=1,2,\cdots
) admits a smooth, spherically symmetric solution
(\overline{Q},\,\overline{U})
. For the range of
\delta>0
, in order to make sure that
\delta_{\rm dis}>0
, it is required that

0<\delta<\frac{\Lambda_{n}(\gamma+1)-2\gamma}{2(\Lambda_{n}-1)}<\frac{\Lambda^{*}(\gamma)(\gamma+1)-2\gamma}{2(\Lambda^{*}(\gamma)-1)}:=\delta^{*}(\gamma)<\frac{1}{2}.

Since
\lim_{n\to\infty}\Lambda_{n}=\Lambda^{*}(\gamma)
, when
\gamma\in(1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}})\setminus\mathcal{J}
is fixed, for any
\delta\in(0,\delta^{*}(\gamma))
, there exists a suitable
\Lambda_{n}\in\{\Lambda_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
such that, taking
\Lambda=\Lambda_{n}
, we have

\delta_{\rm dis}(\gamma,\delta,\Lambda)=\frac{2(1-\delta)(\Lambda-1)}{\gamma-1}+\Lambda-2>0.

Consequently, the assumptions for parameters
(\gamma,\Lambda,\delta)
required in Theorem
2.1
are satisfied. By an analogous argument in Step 1, we can establish the singularity formation of solutions to
CNS
.

9.
Remarks on the Periodic Problem

This section is devoted to the proof of the development of implosions for solutions
of the periodic problem stated in Theorem
1.2
.
The argument is largely analogous to that of Theorem
1.1
.
Accordingly, we provide only a brief outline of the proof and highlight
the minor differences between the Cauchy and the periodic settings.

9.1.
Reformulation of the Periodic Problem

First, problem (
1.22
) can be rewritten as

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}c=-u\cdot\nabla_{x}c-\alpha c\,\mathrm{div}_{x}u\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \qquad\qquad\ \ \qquad\qquad\ \qquad\qquad\ \ \ \quad\ \ \ \text{in $\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}$},\\[6.0pt]
\partial_{t}u=-u\cdot\nabla_{x}u-\alpha c\nabla_{x}c+\alpha^{\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}}c^{\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}}L_{x}u+\frac{\delta}{\alpha}\alpha^{\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}}c^{\frac{\delta-1-\alpha}{\alpha}}\nabla_{x}c\cdot\mathbb{D}_{x}(u)\quad\ \ \ \text{in $\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}$},\\[6.0pt]
(c,u)(0,x)=(c_{0},u_{0})(x):=(\alpha^{-1}\rho^{\alpha}_{0},u_{0})(x)\ \ \ \ \qquad\qquad\ \ \qquad\qquad\ \qquad\quad\text{in $\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}$},\end{cases}

(9.1)

where
(L_{x}u,\mathbb{D}_{x}(u))
are given in (
2.9
),
\alpha=\frac{\gamma-1}{2}
,
and
c=\frac{1}{\alpha}\rho^{\alpha}
.

Second, based on the self-similar scaling (
2.10
)–(
2.11
),
in the self-similar variables

(\tau,y)\in\Omega(\tau_{0}):=\{(\tau,y):\ \tau\in[\tau_{0},\infty),\ y\in e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}\},

(
9.1
) can be rewritten as

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}Q=-(\Lambda-1)Q-(y+U)\cdot\nabla Q-\alpha Q\,\mathrm{div}U\qquad\ \ \ \ \ \ \quad\ \ \ \text{in $\Omega(\tau_{0})$},\\[6.0pt]
\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}U=-(\Lambda-1)U-(y+U)\cdot\nabla U-\alpha Q\nabla Q\\[6.0pt]
\qquad\quad\,+C_{\rm{dis}}e^{-\delta_{\rm{dis}}\tau}\big(Q^{\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}}L(U)+\frac{\delta}{\alpha}Q^{\frac{\delta-1-\alpha}{\alpha}}\nabla Q\cdot\mathbb{D}(U)\big)\qquad\text{in $\Omega(\tau_{0})$},\\[6.0pt]
\displaystyle(Q,U)(\tau_{0},y)=(Q_{0},U_{0})(y)\\[6.0pt]
\ \ \ \ \ \ :=(\Lambda\alpha^{-1}e^{-(\Lambda-1)\tau_{0}}\rho_{0}^{\alpha},\ \Lambda e^{-(\Lambda-1)\tau_{0}}u_{0})(e^{-\tau_{0}}y)\quad\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{in}\ e^{\tau_{0}}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3},\end{cases}

(9.2)

where
(L(U),\mathbb{D}(U))
and
(C_{\rm{dis}},\delta_{\rm{dis}})
are given in (
2.13
)–(
2.14
).

The original problem (
9.1
) is defined in the fixed periodic domain

\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}=\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})^{\top}:-5\leq x_{i}<5,\ i=1,2,3\}.

Under the self-similar scaling of variables
y=e^{\tau}x
, the spatial domain corresponding to the rescaled variable
y
becomes time-dependent,
i.e.
,

e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}=\big\{(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})^{\top}:\,-5e^{\tau}\leq y_{i}<5e^{\tau},\ i=1,2,3\big\}.

Let
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
be the
C^{\infty}
self-similar profile solving (
1.16
) obtained in Lemma
B.2
(see Appendix
B
) when the scaling parameter
\Lambda
introduced in (
1.10
)–(
1.11
) satisfies (
1.13
)–(
1.14
). Since
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})

is spherically symmetric, then there exists a scalar function
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
such that

\overline{Q}(y)=\overline{Q}(r),\quad\overline{U}(y)=\overline{\mathcal{U}}(r)\frac{y}{r}\qquad\,\,\mbox{ for $r=|y|$}.

The cutoff self-similar profile
(\widehat{X}\overline{Q},\widehat{X}\overline{U})
with
\widehat{X}
introduced in (
2.6
) is compactly supported in
B(0,e^{\tau})\subset e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}
. Since its support is strictly contained in the interior of the periodic domain, we may regard it as a periodic function on
e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}
via periodic extension. Consequently, it satisfies the periodic boundary condition automatically.

Motivated by the proof for Theorem
1.1
,

•

the study on the development of implosions for the original periodic problem of
CNS
can also be reduced to a stability analysis around the cutoff self-similar profile
(\widehat{X}\overline{Q},\widehat{X}\overline{U})
.

Based on this viewpoint, the key issue is to analyze the mathematical structure satisfied by the corresponding perturbation

\widetilde{Q}=Q-\widehat{X}\overline{Q},\quad\quad\widetilde{U}=U-\widehat{X}\overline{U}.

(9.3)

According to (
1.16
) and the equations in (
9.2
),

(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})
satisfies the following
equations:

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\widetilde{Q}=

\displaystyle\underbrace{-(\Lambda-1)\widetilde{Q}-(y+\widehat{X}\overline{U})\cdot\nabla\widetilde{Q}-\alpha(\widehat{X}\overline{Q})\,\mathrm{div}\,\widetilde{U}-\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla(\widehat{X}\overline{Q})-\alpha\widetilde{Q}\,\mathrm{div}(\widehat{X}\overline{U})}_{\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}^{e}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})}

\displaystyle\underbrace{-\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{Q}-\alpha\widetilde{Q}\,\mathrm{div}\,\widetilde{U}}_{\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})}

(9.4)

\displaystyle\underbrace{-(\widehat{X}^{2}-\widehat{X})\overline{U}\cdot\nabla\overline{Q}-(1+\alpha)\widehat{X}\overline{U}\cdot\nabla\widehat{X}\overline{Q}-(\widehat{X}^{2}-\widehat{X})\overline{Q}\,\mathrm{div}\,\overline{U}}_{\mathcal{E}_{\zeta}},

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\widetilde{U}=

\displaystyle\underbrace{-(\Lambda-1)\widetilde{U}-(y+\widehat{X}\overline{U})\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U}-\alpha(\widehat{X}\overline{Q})\nabla\widetilde{Q}-\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla(\widehat{X}\overline{U})-\alpha\widetilde{Q}\nabla(\widehat{X}\overline{Q})}_{\mathcal{L}_{u}^{e}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})}

\displaystyle\underbrace{-\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U}-\alpha\widetilde{Q}\nabla\widetilde{Q}}_{\mathcal{N}_{u}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{U})}

(9.5)

\displaystyle\underbrace{-(\widehat{X}^{2}-\widehat{X})\overline{U}\cdot\nabla\overline{U}-\widehat{X}\overline{U}\cdot\nabla\widehat{X}\overline{U}-\alpha(\widehat{X}^{2}-\widehat{X})\overline{Q}\nabla\overline{Q}-\alpha\widehat{X}\overline{Q}\nabla\widehat{X}\overline{Q}}_{\mathcal{E}_{u}}

\displaystyle\underbrace{+C_{\rm{dis}}e^{-\delta_{\rm{dis}}\tau}\big(Q^{\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}}L(U)+\frac{\delta}{\alpha}Q^{\frac{\delta-1-\alpha}{\alpha}}\nabla Q\cdot\mathbb{D}(U)\big)}_{\mathcal{F}_{\rm{dis}}}.

We are now ready to state the desired global stability for problem (
9.2
).

Theorem 9.1
.

We assume that

1<\gamma<1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}
,
\Lambda\in(1,\Lambda^{*}(\gamma))
is the scaling parameter introduced in
Lemma
B.1
such that there exists a smooth, spherically symmetric self-similar profile
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
solving (
1.16
),
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
satisfies
\delta_{\rm dis}>0
,

(m,J)
are two sufficiently large constants determined in
Lemma
C.3
, and
C_{0}>1
is a sufficiently large constant depending on profile
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
and parameter
\Lambda
.
Then there exist positive constants
\nu_{1},E
, and
K
, which depend only on
(m,J)
, and satisfy

K\geq 4,\qquad\nu_{1}\ll\frac{1}{E}\ll\frac{1}{K}\ll 1,

such that,
if the initial data function
(Q_{0},U_{0})
satisfies

\inf_{y\in e^{\tau_{0}}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3}}Q_{0}>0,\quad Q_{0}-Q^{*}\in H^{K}(e^{\tau_{0}}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3}),\quad U_{0}\in H^{K}(e^{\tau_{0}}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3}),

and

\displaystyle\max\big\{\|Q_{0}-\widehat{X}\overline{Q}\|_{L^{\infty}(e^{\tau_{0}}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3})},\ \|U_{0}-\widehat{X}\overline{U}\|_{L^{\infty}(e^{\tau_{0}}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3})}\big\}\leq\nu_{1},

(9.6)

\displaystyle Q_{0}\geq\frac{\nu_{1}}{2},\quad\langle y\rangle^{\Lambda}\big(|\nabla Q_{0}|+|\nabla U_{0}|\big)<\infty,

(9.7)

\displaystyle\max\big\{\|P_{\rm sta}(\chi_{2}(Q_{0}-\widehat{X}\overline{Q}))\|_{X},\ \|P_{\rm sta}(\chi_{2}(U_{0}-\widehat{X}\overline{U}))\|_{X}\big\}\leq\nu_{1},

(9.8)

\displaystyle E_{K}(\tau_{0})=\int_{e^{\tau_{0}}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3}}\big(|\nabla^{K}Q(\tau_{0},y)|^{2}+|\nabla^{K}U(\tau_{0},y)|^{2}\big)\phi^{K}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\frac{E}{2},

(9.9)

where the function space
X
is defined in (
2.3
) based on the choice of
(m,C_{0})
,
and the weight function
\phi\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is defined in (
2.7
),
then the periodic problem (
9.2
) admits a unique global-in-time smooth solution
(Q,U)(\tau,y)
in
\Omega(\tau_{0})
,
which satisfies

\begin{split}&\inf_{y\in e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3}}Q(\tau,y)>0,\ \ Q-Q^{*}\in C([\tau_{0},\tau];H^{K}(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3})),\\
&\partial_{\tau}Q\in C([\tau_{0},\tau];H^{K-1}(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3})),\ \ U\in C([\tau_{0},\tau];H^{K}(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3}))\cap L^{2}([\tau_{0},\tau];H^{K+1}(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3})),\\[2.0pt]
&\partial_{\tau}U\in C([\tau_{0},\tau];H^{K-2}(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3}))\cap L^{2}([\tau_{0},\tau];H^{K-1}(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3})),\end{split}

(9.10)

and

\max\big\{\|Q-\widehat{X}\overline{Q}\|_{L^{\infty}(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3})},\ \|U-\widehat{X}\overline{U}\|_{L^{\infty}(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3})}\big\}\leq\frac{\nu_{0}}{100}e^{-\varepsilon(\tau-\tau_{0})},\qquad E_{K}(\tau)\leq E,

(9.11)

for all
\tau>\tau_{0}
, where
\nu_{0}>0
is a sufficiently small positive constant depending only on
E
, and
\varepsilon>0
is a small positive constant depending only on
\delta_{\rm dis}
, so that they satisfy

\nu_{0}^{3/2}\ll\nu_{1}\ll\nu_{0}\ll\frac{1}{E}\ll\frac{1}{K}\ll\frac{1}{m}\ll\varepsilon\ll 1.

Moreover, there exists a finite-codimension set of initial data satisfying the above conditions
(
see
Appendix
D

for more details
)
.

9.2.
Local-In-Time Well-Posedness of the Reformulated Problem

The desired local-in-time well-posedness in this section can be stated as follows:

Theorem 9.2
.

Assume that parameters
(\Lambda,\alpha,\delta,a_{1},a_{2})
satisfy (
3.1
)
and
K\geq 4
is an integer.
Let the initial data
(Q_{0},U_{0})
satisfy

\inf_{y\in e^{\tau_{0}}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3}}Q_{0}(y)>0,\quad Q_{0}-Q^{*}\in H^{s}(e^{\tau_{0}}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3}),\quad U_{0}\in H^{s}(e^{\tau_{0}}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3})

(9.12)

for some integer
s\geq K
. Then there exists
\tau_{*}>\tau_{0}
such that problem (
9.2
) admits a unique smooth solution
(Q,U)(\tau,y)
in

\Omega(\tau_{0},\tau_{*})=\big\{(\tau,y):\ \tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{*}],\ y\in e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}\big\},

which satisfies

\begin{split}&\inf_{(\tau,y)\in\Omega(\tau_{0},\tau_{*})}Q(\tau,y)>0,\ \ Q-Q^{*}\in C([\tau_{0},\tau_{*}];H^{s}(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3})),\\
&\partial_{\tau}Q\in C([\tau_{0},\tau_{*}];H^{s-1}(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3})),\ \ U\in C([\tau_{0},\tau_{*}];H^{s}(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3}))\cap L^{2}([\tau_{0},\tau_{*}];H^{s+1}(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3})),\\[2.0pt]
&\partial_{\tau}U\in C([\tau_{0},\tau_{*}];H^{s-2}(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3}))\cap L^{2}([\tau_{0},\tau_{*}];H^{s-1}(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}_{10}^{3})).\end{split}

(9.13)

Moreover, if
E_{K}(\tau_{0})<\infty
, then

\text{\rm ess}\!\!\!\sup_{\tau_{0}\leq\tau\leq\tau_{*}}E_{K}(\tau)<\infty.

(9.14)

The corresponding local-in-time well-posedness of smooth solutions can be obtained by a similar argument as in §
3
. Moreover, since the weight function
\phi(y)
is bounded in
e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}
, then the boundedness of the weighted energy
E_{K}(\tau)
follows from the
H^{s}
–regularity of solutions.

9.3.
Global-In-Time Stability of the Reformulated Problem

We assume that all the related parameters still satisfy (
4.1
)–(
4.2
). Since
\tau_{0}
is sufficiently large such that

C(\nu_{0})\leq e^{\delta_{\rm dis}\tau_{0}},

it follows that, for all
\tau\geq\tau_{0}
,

B(0,C_{0})\Subset e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10},\qquad B(0,R_{0})\Subset e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}.

(9.15)

Consequently, we may apply the same region segmentation method as in the Cauchy problem to establish the global-in-time stability for the periodic problem. The proof is analogous except the following two points:

•

When the interpolation estimates are needed, we employ the Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation
inequalities on the periodic domain
e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}
, as stated in
Lemmas
A.5
–
A.6
.

•

Since the periodic domain
e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}
depends on time,
differentiating the energy functional with respect to
\tau
generates an additional boundary
term associated with the moving boundary.
Moreover, to control the higher-order derivatives in certain integrals,
one must perform integration by parts. When the integrand contains non-periodic components,
such as the spatial variable
y
, additional new boundary terms may arise
on
\partial(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10})
, which do not appear in the Cauchy problem.
A direct computation shows that the boundary terms generated by integration by parts
exactly cancel those arising from the time differentiation of the expanding domain.
Consequently, the same energy estimates as in the Cauchy problem remain valid in the periodic setting.

9.3.1.
4
-th order derivative estimates in
B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}

To control

\|\nabla^{4}\widetilde{Q}\|_{L^{2}(B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10})}+\|\nabla^{4}\widetilde{U}\|_{L^{2}(B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10})},

applying
\partial_{\beta}
with multi-index
\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3},\beta_{4})
satisfying
|\beta|=4
to (
9.4
)–(
9.5
), we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}

\displaystyle=B_{\zeta,0}(\widetilde{U},\widetilde{Q})+B_{\zeta,1}(\widetilde{U},\widetilde{Q})+B_{\zeta,2}(\widetilde{U},\widetilde{Q})+B_{\zeta,3}(\widetilde{U},\widetilde{Q})+\partial_{\beta}\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}+\partial_{\beta}\mathcal{E}_{\zeta},

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}

\displaystyle=B_{u,0}(\widetilde{U},\widetilde{Q})+B_{u,1}(\widetilde{U},\widetilde{Q})+B_{u,2}(\widetilde{U},\widetilde{Q})+B_{u,3}(\widetilde{U},\widetilde{Q})+\partial_{\beta}\mathcal{N}_{u}+\partial_{\beta}\mathcal{E}_{u}+\partial_{\beta}\mathcal{F}_{\rm dis},

where, for
0\leq i\leq 3
,
B_{\zeta,i}
and
B_{u,i}
denote the collections of the terms as
defined in Step 1 of the proof of Lemma
6.5
.

Multiplying the above equations by
\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}
and

\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}
respectively, summing over all

|\beta|=4
, and then integrating over
B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}
, we obtain from (
9.15
) that

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\Big(\|\nabla^{4}\widetilde{Q}\|_{L^{2}(B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10})}^{2}+\|\nabla^{4}\widetilde{U}\|_{L^{2}(B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10})}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\sum_{|\beta|=4}\int_{B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}\big(|\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}|^{2}+|\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}|^{2}\big)\,\text{d}y

\displaystyle=\sum_{|\beta|=4}\int_{B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\big(|\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}|^{2}+|\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}|^{2}\big)\,\text{d}y

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{|\beta|=4}5e^{\tau}\int_{\partial(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10})}\big(|\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}|^{2}+|\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}|^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}S

\displaystyle=2\sum_{|\beta|=4}\sum_{j=0}^{3}\bigg(\int_{B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}\cdot B_{u,j}\text{d}y+\int_{B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}\,B_{\zeta,j}\,\text{d}y\bigg)

(9.16)

\displaystyle\quad+2\sum_{|\beta|=4}\bigg(\int_{B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}\cdot\partial_{\beta}\mathcal{N}_{u}\text{d}y+\int_{B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}\,\partial_{\beta}\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}\,\text{d}y\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+2\sum_{|\beta|=4}\bigg(\int_{B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}\cdot\partial_{\beta}\mathcal{E}_{u}\,\text{d}y+\int_{B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}\,\partial_{\beta}\mathcal{E}_{\zeta}\,\text{d}y\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+2\sum_{|\beta|=4}\int_{B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}\cdot\partial_{\beta}\mathcal{F}_{\rm dis}\,\text{d}y

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\sum_{|\beta|=4}5e^{\tau}\int_{\partial(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10})}\big(|\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}|^{2}+|\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}|^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}S}_{I^{c}_{b}:\ \text{boundary term of the periodic domain}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{|\beta|=4}\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{3}I_{j,\beta}+I_{N,\beta}+I_{E,\beta}+I_{F,\beta}\Big)+I^{c}_{b}.

For
I_{0,\beta}
, it follows from integration by parts, the definition of
\widehat{X}
, (
4.1
), (
4.17
), (
B.5
), and Lemma
6.3
that

\displaystyle I_{0,\beta}

\displaystyle=-2\int_{B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}\Big(\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}\cdot\big((y+\widehat{X}\overline{U})\cdot\nabla\big)\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}+(\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla)\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}(\alpha\widehat{X}\overline{Q})\Big)\,\text{d}y

\displaystyle\quad-2\int_{B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}\Big(\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}(y+\widehat{X}\overline{U})\cdot\nabla\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}+\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}\,\mathrm{div}(\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U})\,\alpha\widehat{X}\overline{Q}\Big)\,\text{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}\big(3+|\mathrm{div}(\widehat{X}\overline{U})|\big)\big(|\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}|^{2}+|\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}|^{2}\big)\,\text{d}y

\displaystyle\quad+2\int_{B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}|\nabla(\alpha\widehat{X}\overline{Q})||\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}||\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}|\,\text{d}y

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\partial B(0,C_{0})}\big(C_{0}+|\widehat{X}\overline{\mathcal{U}}|\big)\big(|\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}|^{2}+|\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}|^{2}\big)\,\text{d}S

\displaystyle\quad+2\int_{\partial B(0,C_{0})}|\alpha\widehat{X}\overline{Q}||\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}||\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}|\text{d}S-5\int_{\partial(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10})}\big(|\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}|^{2}+|\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}|^{2}\big)e^{\tau}\,\text{d}S

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\partial B(0,C_{0})}\big(3+3|\nabla(\widehat{X}\overline{U})|+|\nabla(\alpha\widehat{X}\overline{Q})|\big)\big(|\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}|^{2}+|\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}|^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}y+(\frac{\nu_{1}}{\nu_{g}})^{\frac{17}{10}}e^{-2\varepsilon(\tau-\tau_{0})}

\displaystyle\quad-\underbrace{5e^{\tau}\int_{\partial(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10})}\big(|\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{Q}|^{2}+|\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{U}|^{2}\big)\,\text{d}S.}_{I^{\beta}_{bc}:\ \text{boundary term of the periodic domain}}

It is observed that, after summing over all
|\beta|=4
,

I^{c}_{b}-\sum_{|\beta|=4}I^{\beta}_{bc}=0,

which yields that
the corresponding boundary terms arising from integration by parts cancel out.
Moreover, the estimates for all remaining terms are identical to those in the Cauchy problem.

9.3.2.
K
-th order weighted energy estimate

Let
\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\cdots,\beta_{K})
with
|\beta|=K
.
For the periodic problem (
9.2
), we denote the
K
-th order weighted energy by

E_{K}(\tau)=\sum_{|\beta|=K}\int_{e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}\big(|\partial_{\beta}Q|^{2}+|\partial_{\beta}U|^{2}\big)\phi^{K}\,\mathrm{d}y.

(9.17)

Applying
\partial_{\beta}
to (
9.2
) and using the fact that

\partial_{\beta}(y\cdot\nabla f)=K\partial_{\beta}f+y\cdot\nabla\partial_{\beta}f,

we have

\displaystyle(\partial_{\tau}+\Lambda-1+K)\partial_{\beta}Q+y\cdot\nabla\partial_{\beta}Q+\partial_{\beta}(U\cdot\nabla Q)+\alpha\partial_{\beta}(Q\,\mathrm{div}U)=0,

(9.18)

\displaystyle(\partial_{\tau}+\Lambda-1+K)\partial_{\beta}U+y\cdot\nabla\partial_{\beta}U+\partial_{\beta}(U\cdot\nabla U)+\alpha\partial_{\beta}(Q\nabla Q)=\partial_{\beta}\mathcal{F}_{\rm dis}.

Multiplying each equation of (
9.18
) by
\phi^{K}\partial_{\beta}Q
and
\phi^{K}\partial_{\beta}U
respectively, summing over all
|\beta|=K
, and integrating over
e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\big(\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}+\Lambda-1+K\big)E_{K}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{|\beta|=K}\int_{e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}\phi^{K}y\big(\partial_{\beta}Q\nabla\partial_{\beta}Q+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\partial_{\beta}U_{i}\nabla\partial_{\beta}U_{i}\big)\,\text{d}y

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{|\beta|=K}\int_{e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}\phi^{K}\big(\partial_{\beta}Q\,\partial_{\beta}(U\cdot\nabla Q)+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\partial_{\beta}U_{i}\,\partial_{\beta}(U\cdot\nabla U_{i})\big)\,\text{d}y

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{|\beta|=K}\int_{e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}\alpha\phi^{K}\big(\partial_{\beta}Q\,\partial_{\beta}(Q\,\mathrm{div}\,U)+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\partial_{\beta}U_{i}\,\partial_{\beta}(Q\partial_{y_{i}}Q)\big)\,\text{d}y

(9.19)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{|\beta|=K}C_{\rm{dis}}e^{-\delta_{\rm{dis}}\tau}\int_{e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}\phi^{K}\partial_{\beta}U\,\partial_{\beta}\big(Q^{\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}}L(U)+\frac{\delta}{\alpha}Q^{\frac{\delta-1}{\alpha}-1}\nabla Q\cdot\mathbb{D}(U)\big)\,\text{d}y

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\frac{5}{2}e^{\tau}\sum_{|\beta|=K}\int_{\partial(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10})}\big(|\partial_{\beta}Q|^{2}+|\partial_{\beta}U|^{2}\big)\phi^{K}\,\text{d}S}_{I^{\phi}_{b}:\ \text{boundary term of the periodic domain}}

\displaystyle=-I_{1}-I_{2}-I_{3}+C_{\rm{dis}}e^{-\delta_{\rm{dis}}\tau}I_{4}+I^{\phi}_{b}.

For
I_{1}
, via integration by parts, it follows that

\displaystyle-I_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{|\beta|=K}\int_{e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}\phi^{K}y\big(\nabla|\partial_{\beta}Q|^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\nabla|\partial_{\beta}U_{i}|^{2}\big)\,\text{d}y

(9.20)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{|\beta|=K}\int_{e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10}}\frac{\mathrm{div}(\phi^{K}y)}{\phi^{K}}\big(|\partial_{\beta}Q|^{2}+|\partial_{\beta}U|^{2}\big)\phi^{K}\,\text{d}y

\displaystyle\underbrace{-\frac{5}{2}e^{\tau}\sum_{|\beta|=K}\int_{\partial(e^{\tau}\mathbb{T}^{3}_{10})}\big(|\partial_{\beta}Q|^{2}+|\partial_{\beta}U|^{2}\big)\phi^{K}\,\text{d}S}_{I^{1}_{b\phi}:\ \text{boundary term of the periodic domain}}.

It is observed that

I^{\phi}_{b}-I^{1}_{b\phi}=0,

which yields that
the corresponding boundary terms arising from integration by parts cancel out.
In all remaining integrations by parts, no boundary terms appear due to the periodic boundary conditions.

9.4.
Development of Implosions of Solutions of the Original Periodic Problem

Based on the global-in-time stability for problem (
9.2
) obtained in Theorem
9.1
, the corresponding development of implosions of solutions of the periodic problem (
1.22
)
follows exactly the same argument used in the Cauchy problem, which can be found in §
8
.

Appendix A
Basic Lemmas

This appendix lists some useful lemmas that have been used frequently in the previous sections.
The first lemma gives the well-known Fatou’s lemma (
cf
.
[
41
]
)

Lemma A.1
.

Given a measure space
(V,\mathcal{H},\varkappa)
and a set
\beth\in\mathcal{H}
,
let
\{f_{n}\}
be a sequence of
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}})
–measurable
non-negative functions
f_{n}(\upsilon):\beth\rightarrow[0,\infty]
. Define a function
f(\upsilon):\beth\rightarrow[0,\infty]
by setting

f(\upsilon)=\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}f_{n}(\upsilon)\qquad\mbox{for every $\upsilon\in\beth$}.

Then
f
is
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}})
–measurable and

\int_{\beth}f(\upsilon)\,\text{\rm d}\varkappa\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\beth}f_{n}(\upsilon)\,\text{\rm d}\varkappa.

The second lemma shows the weak compactness in weighted Sobolev spaces for a suitably chosen weight function.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\phi
be a weighted function defined in (
2.7
),
and let
\{f_{n}(y)\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence with
f_{n}(y):\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
.
Suppose that, for some constant
\bar{C}_{1},\bar{C}_{2}>1
and for all
n\geq 1
,

\|f_{n}\|^{2}_{H^{K}}\leq\bar{C}_{1},

(A.1)

and for the multi-index
\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\cdots,\beta_{K})
with

\beta_{i}\in\{1,2,3\}
for
i=1,\cdots,K
,

\sum_{|\beta|=K}\int|\partial_{\beta}f_{n}|^{2}\phi^{K}\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\bar{C}_{2},

(A.2)

where
\bar{C}_{1},\bar{C}_{2}
, and
K
are independent of
n
and
\phi
.
Then there exist a subsequence
(
still denoted by
n
)

and functions
f\in H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
F_{\beta}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that

\displaystyle f_{n}\to f

\displaystyle\textit{weakly in $H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3})$},

(A.3)

\displaystyle\partial_{\beta}f_{n}\phi^{\frac{K}{2}}\to F_{\beta}

\displaystyle\textit{weakly in $L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})$}.

Moreover, for every multi-index
\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\cdots,\beta_{K})
with

\beta_{i}\in\{1,2,3\}
for
i=1,\cdots,K
, the weak limits in (
A.3
) are uniquely identified by

F_{\beta}=\partial_{\beta}f\phi^{\frac{K}{2}}\quad\text{ in $L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})$}.

(A.4)

Proof.

We divide the proof into two steps.

1.
Local weak limits and identification.

Fix
R>0
and a multi-index
\beta
with
|\beta|=K
.
It follows from the uniform bounds in (
A.1
) that

\|f_{n}\|_{H^{K}(B(0,R))}\leq\|f_{n}\|_{H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq\bar{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{1}.

Then there exist both a subsequence (still denoted by
n
) and a function
f^{(R)}\in H^{K}(B(0,R))
such
that

f_{n}\to f^{(R)}\qquad\text{weakly in $H^{K}(B(0,R))$}.

In particular, for every multi-index
\beta
with
|\beta|=K
, we also have

\partial_{\beta}f_{n}\to\partial_{\beta}f^{(R)}\qquad\text{weakly in $L^{2}(B(0,R))$}.

(A.5)

Next, the weighted energy bound (
A.2
) implies

\|\partial_{\beta}f_{n}\phi^{\frac{K}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(B(0,R))}\leq\|\partial_{\beta}f_{n}\phi^{\frac{K}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq\bar{C}_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

After extracting a further subsequence (still denoted by
n
), there exists
F_{\beta}^{(R)}\in L^{2}(B(0,R))
such that

\partial_{\beta}f_{n}\phi^{\frac{K}{2}}\to F_{\beta}^{(R)}\qquad\text{weakly in $L^{2}(B(0,R))$}.

(A.6)

By the definition of the weight function
\phi
in (
2.7
), we see that

\phi^{\frac{K}{2}}\in L^{\infty}(B(0,R))
.
Since the bounded linear operators preserve weak convergence,
the weak convergence (
A.5
) implies

\partial_{\beta}f_{n}\phi^{\frac{K}{2}}\to\partial_{\beta}f^{(R)}\phi^{\frac{K}{2}}\qquad\text{weakly in $L^{2}(B(0,R))$}.

By the uniqueness of weak limits in
L^{2}(B(0,R))
, we conclude

F_{\beta}^{(R)}=\partial_{\beta}f^{(R)}\phi^{\frac{K}{2}}\qquad\text{ in $L^{2}(B(0,R))$}.

(A.7)

2.
Diagonal subsequence and consistency of local limits.

Perform the above extraction for
R=1,2,3,\cdots
and use a diagonal argument to obtain a subsequence
\{f_{n}\}
such that, for every
R\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle f_{n}\to f^{(R)}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{weakly in $H^{K}(B(0,R))$},

\displaystyle\partial_{\beta}f_{n}\phi^{\frac{K}{2}}\to F^{(R)}_{\beta}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{weakly in $L^{2}(B(0,R))$}.

For
1\leq R<R^{*}
, the restriction map from
H^{K}(B(0,R^{*}))
to
H^{K}(B(0,R))
is bounded and linear, hence preserves weak convergence. Therefore, it follows from the uniqueness of weak limits that

f^{(R^{*})}|_{B(0,R)}=f^{(R)}\qquad\text{{\it a.e.} in $B(0,R)$}.

This compatibility allows us to define a global function
f\in H^{K}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
by setting
f=f^{(R)}
in
B(0,R)
. Combining this with the local identification obtained in Step 1, we conclude that, for every
R
,

F_{\beta}^{(R)}=\partial_{\beta}f\,\phi^{\frac{K}{2}}\qquad\text{in $L^{2}(B(0,R))$}.

Similarly, we also define a global function
F_{\beta}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
by setting
F_{\beta}=F^{(R)}_{\beta}
in
B(0,R)
. Since
R>0
is arbitrary, we derive

F_{\beta}=\partial_{\beta}f\phi^{\frac{K}{2}}\qquad\text{ in $L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})$}.

∎

The next lemma provides a weighted Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequality,
which plays an important role in our analysis.

Lemma A.3

(
[
5
]
)
.

Let
f(y):\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
I_{-1}=[0,1]
, and
I_{j}=[2^{j},2^{j+1}]
,
and let
\varphi(y),\psi(y)>0
be two weight functions such that there exist values
\varphi_{j},\psi_{j}
,
and a constant
C^{*}
satisfying
\varphi(y)\in[\varphi_{j}/C^{*},C^{*}\varphi_{j}]

and
\psi(y)\in[\psi_{j}/C^{*},C^{*}\psi_{j}]
for all
y
with
|y|\in I_{j}
.
Moreover, assume that
\varphi(y)
and
\psi(y)
are approximately
1
at the origin, that is,

\varphi_{-1}=\psi_{-1}=\varphi_{0}=\psi_{0}=1.

For an integer
1\leq i\leq l
, assume that parameters
(p,q,\bar{r},\theta)
satisfy

\frac{1}{\bar{r}}=\frac{i}{3}+\theta\Big(\frac{1}{q}-\frac{l}{3}\Big)+\frac{1-\theta}{p},\qquad\frac{i}{l}\leq\theta<1.

(A.8)

When
\bar{r}=\infty
, then

|\nabla^{i}f|\leq C_{3}\big(\|\psi^{l}f\|_{L^{p}}^{1-\theta}\|\varphi^{l}\nabla^{l}f\|_{L^{q}}^{\theta}\psi^{-l(1-\theta)}\varphi^{-l\theta}+\|\psi^{l}f\|_{L^{p}}\,\langle y\rangle^{3\theta(\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{p})-l\theta}\psi^{-l}\big).

(A.9)

Moreover, if
p=\infty
,
q=2
, and
\psi=1
, then

\left|\nabla^{i}f\right|\leq C_{4}\big(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}^{1-\frac{i}{l-3/2}}\|\varphi^{l}\nabla^{l}f\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{i}{l-3/2}}\varphi^{-l\frac{i}{l-3/2}}+\left\|f\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\,\langle y\rangle^{-i}\big).

(A.10)

Here
C_{3}
and
C_{4}
are both positive constants that depend on
(p,q,i,l,\theta,C^{*})
,
but are independent of
(f,\psi,\varphi)
.

When
\bar{r}\in[1,\infty)
, given any
\bar{\varepsilon}>0
, under the extra assumption
:

\left(\frac{\varphi(y)}{\langle y\rangle\psi(y)}\right)^{l\theta}\langle y\rangle^{3\theta(\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{p})}\leq C_{5},

(A.11)

the following weighted Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality holds
:

\|\langle y\rangle^{-\bar{\varepsilon}}\psi^{l(1-\theta)}\varphi^{l\theta}\nabla^{i}f\|_{L^{\bar{r}}}\leq C_{6}\big(\|\psi^{l}f\|_{L^{p}}^{1-\theta}\|\varphi^{l}\nabla^{l}f\|_{L^{q}}^{\theta}+\|\psi^{l}f\|_{L^{p}}\big),

(A.12)

where
C_{5}
is a positive constant depending on
(p,q,l,\theta,C^{*})
, while
C_{6}
depends on parameters

(p,q,i,l,\theta,\bar{r},\bar{\varepsilon},C^{*})
, but is independent of
(f,\psi,\varphi)
.

Moreover, in the exterior region
B^{c}(0,C_{0})
for some constant
C_{0}\geq 1
, the following interpolation inequality holds:

Lemma A.4

(
[
5
]
)
.

Let
f(y):\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
be in
H^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, and
let
C_{0}\geq 1
be a constant independent of
f
. Assume that parameters
(p,q,\bar{r},\theta,l)
satisfy

\frac{1}{\bar{r}}=\frac{i}{3}+\theta\Big(\frac{1}{q}-\frac{l}{3}\Big)+\frac{1-\theta}{p},\qquad\frac{i}{l}\leq\theta<1,\qquad\frac{l}{3}\geq\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{p}.

(A.13)

Then the Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequalities hold

\|\nabla^{i}f\|_{L^{\bar{r}}(B^{c}(0,C_{0}))}\leq C_{7}\big(\|f\|_{L^{p}(B^{c}(0,C_{0}))}^{1-\theta}\|\nabla^{l}f\|_{L^{q}(B^{c}(0,C_{0}))}^{\theta}+\|f\|_{L^{p}(B^{c}(0,C_{0}))}\big),

(A.14)

where
C_{7}
is a positive constant that depends on
(p,q,i,l,\theta,\bar{r})
, but is independent of
f
and
C_{0}
.

We next state the corresponding weighted Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequalities on the periodic domain
\mathbb{T}^{3}_{2\bar{L}}
of period
2\bar{L}
.

Lemma A.5

(
[
5
]
)
.

Let
f(y):\mathbb{T}^{3}_{2\bar{L}}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
\bar{L}\geq 1
,
I_{-1}=[0,1]
,
I_{j}=[2^{j},2^{j+1}]
,
and let
\varphi(y),\psi(y)>0
be two weight functions such that there exist values
\varphi_{j},\psi_{j}
, and a constant
C^{*}

satisfying
\varphi(y)\in[\varphi_{j}/C^{*},C^{*}\varphi_{j}]
and
\psi(y)\in[\psi_{j}/C^{*},C^{*}\psi_{j}]
for all
y
with
|y|\in I_{j}
.
Moreover, assume that
\varphi(y)
and
\psi(y)
are approximately
1
at the origin, that is,

\varphi_{-1}=\psi_{-1}=\varphi_{0}=\psi_{0}=1.

For an integer
1\leq i\leq l
, assume that parameters
(p,q,\bar{r},\theta)
satisfy (
A.8
).

When
\bar{r}=\infty
, then

\left|\nabla^{i}f\right|\leq C_{3}\Big(\|\psi^{l}f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{2\bar{L}})}^{1-\theta}\|\varphi^{l}\nabla^{l}f\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{2\bar{L}})}^{\theta}\psi^{-l(1-\theta)}\varphi^{-l\theta}+\|\psi^{l}f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{2\bar{L}})}\,\langle y\rangle^{3\theta(\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{p})-l\theta}\psi^{-l}\Big).

(A.15)

Moreover, if
p=\infty
,
q=2
, and
\psi=1
, then

\left|\nabla^{i}f\right|\leq C_{4}\Big(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{2\bar{L}})}^{1-\frac{i}{l-3/2}}\|\varphi^{l}\nabla^{l}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{2\bar{L}})}^{\frac{i}{l-3/2}}\varphi^{-l\frac{i}{l-3/2}}+\left\|f\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{2\bar{L}})}\cdot\langle y\rangle^{-i}\Big).

(A.16)

Here
C_{3}
and
C_{4}
are both positive constants that may depend on
(p,q,i,l,\theta,C^{*})
,
but are independent of
(f,\bar{L},\psi,\varphi)
.

When
\bar{r}\in[1,\infty)
, given any
\bar{\varepsilon}>0
, and under the extra assumption (
A.11
),
the following weighted Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality holds
:

\|\langle y\rangle^{-\bar{\varepsilon}}\psi^{l(1-\theta)}\varphi^{l\theta}\nabla^{i}f\|_{L^{\bar{r}}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{2\bar{L}})}\leq C_{6}\Big(\|\psi^{l}f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{2\bar{L}})}^{1-\theta}\|\varphi^{l}\nabla^{l}f\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{2\bar{L}})}^{\theta}+\|\psi^{l}f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{2\bar{L}})}\Big),

(A.17)

where
C_{5}
is a positive constant depending on
(p,q,l,\theta,C^{*})
, while
C_{6}
depends on
parameters
(p,q,i,l,\theta,C^{*},\bar{r},\bar{\varepsilon})
, but is independent of
(f,\bar{L},\psi,\varphi)
.

Moreover, in the exterior region
B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap\mathbb{T}^{3}_{2\bar{L}}
for some constant
C_{0}\geq 1
, we have the following Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequality without weights:

Lemma A.6

(
[
5
]
)
.

Let
f(y):\mathbb{T}^{3}_{2\bar{L}}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
be in
H^{l}(\mathbb{T}^{3}_{2\bar{L}})
with
\bar{L}\geq 10C_{0}
, where
C_{0}\geq 1
is
a constant independent of
f
.
Then, if parameters
(p,q,\bar{r},\theta,l)
satisfy (
A.13
),
the Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequalities hold
:

\begin{split}\|\nabla^{i}f\|_{L^{\bar{r}}(B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap\mathbb{T}^{3}_{2\bar{L}})}&\leq C_{7}\|f\|_{L^{p}(B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap\mathbb{T}^{3}_{2\bar{L}})}^{1-\theta}\|\nabla^{l}f\|_{L^{q}(B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap\mathbb{T}^{3}_{2\bar{L}})}^{\theta}\\
&\quad+C_{7}\|f\|_{L^{p}(B^{c}(0,C_{0})\cap\mathbb{T}^{3}_{2\bar{L}})},\end{split}

(A.18)

where
C_{7}
is a positive constant that depends only on
(p,q,i,l,\theta,\bar{r})
,
but is independent of
(f,\bar{L},C_{0})
.

Appendix B
Self-Similar Solutions to the Steady Compressible Euler Equations

This appendix is devoted to showing some properties of the smooth and spherically symmetric self-similar solution to the steady Euler equations, which have been verified in
[
2
,
37
,
5
,
43
]
. The spherically symmetric self-similar solution

(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
takes the form:

\overline{Q}(y)=\overline{Q}(r),\quad\overline{U}(y)=\overline{\mathcal{U}}(r)\frac{y}{r}\qquad\,\,\mbox{for $r=|y|$}.

Thus, the steady Euler equations (
1.16
) reduce to the following
system of ordinary differential equations

(\overline{Q},\overline{\mathcal{U}})
:

\begin{split}&(\Lambda-1)\overline{Q}+(r+\overline{\mathcal{U}})\partial_{r}\overline{Q}+\alpha\overline{Q}\big(\partial_{r}\overline{\mathcal{U}}+\frac{2}{r}\overline{\mathcal{U}}\big)=0,\\
&(\Lambda-1)\overline{\mathcal{U}}+(r+\overline{\mathcal{U}})\partial_{r}\overline{\mathcal{U}}+\alpha\overline{Q}\,\partial_{r}\overline{Q}=0.\end{split}

(B.1)

Following
[
2
]
and
[
37
]
, one introduces the Emden
transformation:

\overline{Q}(r)=r\,\widehat{Q}(\xi),\quad\overline{\mathcal{U}}(r)=r\,\widehat{U}(\xi)\qquad\,\,\mbox{ for $\xi=\log r$},

(B.2)

which maps (
B.1
) onto the following
autonomous
system:

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\Lambda\widehat{Q}+(1+\widehat{U})\frac{{\rm d}\widehat{Q}}{{\rm d}\xi}+\widehat{Q}\widehat{U}+\alpha\widehat{Q}\big(\frac{d\widehat{U}}{d\xi}+3\widehat{U}\big)=0,\\[12.0pt]
\displaystyle\Lambda\widehat{U}+(1+\widehat{U})\frac{{\rm d}\widehat{U}}{{\rm d}\xi}+\widehat{U}^{2}+\alpha\widehat{Q}\big(\frac{{\rm d}\widehat{Q}}{{\rm d}\xi}+\widehat{Q}\big)=0,\end{cases}\Longleftrightarrow\quad\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}\widehat{Q}}{{\rm d}\xi}=\frac{N_{Q}(\widehat{Q},\widehat{U})}{D(\widehat{Q},\widehat{U})},\\[12.0pt]
\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}\widehat{U}}{{\rm d}\xi}=\frac{N_{U}(\widehat{Q},\widehat{U})}{D(\widehat{Q},\widehat{U})},\end{cases}

(B.3)

where

\displaystyle N_{Q}(\widehat{Q},\widehat{U})

\displaystyle=-(1+\widehat{U})\widehat{Q}(\Lambda+(1+3\alpha)\widehat{U})+\alpha\widehat{Q}(\Lambda\widehat{U}+\widehat{U}^{2}+\alpha\widehat{Q}^{2}),

\displaystyle N_{U}(\widehat{Q},\widehat{U})

\displaystyle=-(1+\widehat{U})(\Lambda\widehat{U}+\widehat{U}^{2}+\alpha\widehat{Q}^{2})+\alpha\widehat{Q}^{2}(\Lambda+(1+3\alpha)\widehat{U}),

\displaystyle D(\widehat{Q},\widehat{U})

\displaystyle=(1+\widehat{U})^{2}-\alpha^{2}\widehat{Q}^{2}.

The compactified phase portrait in the
(\widehat{Q},\widehat{U})
–plane provides a
geometric description of self-similar profiles through the Emden transformation. In particular, a smooth orbit of the autonomous system
(
B.3
) connecting
P_{0}
and
P_{\infty}
corresponds to a smooth spherically symmetric self-similar profile that is regular at the origin and decays at infinity, which is stated in the following two lemmas.

\widehat{Q}
\widehat{U}

Figure 2.
Phase portrait trajectory from
P_{\infty}
to
P_{0}
.

Lemma B.1

(Existence of Smooth Solutions to (
B.3
))
.

Let
\Lambda^{*}(\gamma)
be defined as in (
1.14
).

(i)

There exists a
(
possibly empty
)
exceptional countable set
\mathcal{J}:=\{\gamma_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
whose accumulation points can only be at
\{1,\frac{5}{3},\infty\}
and
\{\frac{7}{5},\frac{5}{3}\}\cap\mathcal{J}=\varnothing
. Then, for each
\gamma\in(1,\infty)\setminus\mathcal{J}
, there exists a discrete sequence of scaling parameters

\{\Lambda_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
satisfying

1<\Lambda_{n}<\Lambda^{*}(\gamma),\qquad\lim_{n\to\infty}\Lambda_{n}=\Lambda^{*}(\gamma),

such that the autonomous system (
B.3
) with
\Lambda=\Lambda_{n}
,
n=1,2,\cdots
, admits a smooth solution connecting
P_{0}
to
P_{\infty}=(0,0)
;

see
[
37
, Theorem 1.3]
,
[
2
, Theorem 1.2]
, and
[
43
, Theorem 1.1]
.

(ii)

Let
\gamma\in(1,\infty)
.
There exists an interval
I_{\gamma}\subsetneq(1,\Lambda^{*}(\gamma))
and a parameter
\Lambda\in I_{\gamma}
such that the autonomous system (
B.3
) admits a smooth solution connecting
P_{0}
to
P_{\infty}=(0,0)
;

see
[
2
, Theorem 1.1]
.

In
Figure
2
,
P_{0}
is a point in the compactified phase portrait, with finite value of
\widehat{U}
and
\widehat{Q}=\infty
, representing
the regular behavior of self-similar profiles at origin
r=0
,
whereas
P_{\infty}=(0,0)
corresponds to the values of profiles at
r=\infty
. Point
P_{s}
is a regular singular point,
and there exist two smooth integral curves crossing
P_{s}
:
one is tangent to the direction
\nu_{-}
and the other one is tangent to
\nu_{+}
. The curve tangent to
\nu_{+}
corresponds to a shock-type self-similar solution to the Euler equations, whereas the curve tangent to
\nu_{-}
yields a smooth self-similar solution to the Euler equations.

While Lemma
B.1
establishes the existence of a smooth orbit in the phase plane, it remains to verify that this orbit corresponds to a smooth profile in the spatial variables. By projecting this orbit back into the physical coordinates via the inverse Emden transformation, we obtain a globally defined, spherically symmetric smooth profile
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
.

Lemma B.2

(Existence of Smooth Self-Similar Euler Profiles)
.

Suppose that parameters
(\gamma,\Lambda)
in the autonomous system (
B.3
) are
chosen according to either case
(i)
or case
(ii)
in
Lemma
B.1
.

In either case, the system admits a smooth solution
(\widehat{Q},\widehat{U})
connecting
P_{0}
to

P_{\infty}=(0,0)
in the
(\widehat{Q},\widehat{U})
–phase plane.
Define

\overline{Q}(y)=|y|\,\widehat{Q}(\xi),\quad\overline{U}(y)=\widehat{U}(\xi)\,y\qquad\,\,\mbox{for $\xi=\log|y|$}.

Then
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
is a smooth, spherically symmetric solution
to the steady Euler system (
1.16
). Moreover, the asymptotic behavior of

(\widehat{Q},\widehat{U})
at
P_{0}
implies

\overline{Q}(0)=Q_{*}>0,\qquad\overline{U}(0)=0,

and that profile
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
is smooth in a
neighborhood of the origin. On the other hand, the convergence of the orbit to

P_{\infty}
yields

(\overline{Q},\overline{U})(y)\to(0,0)\qquad\text{as }|y|\to\infty,

corresponding to the decay of the profile at spatial infinity.

We now summarize the main
properties of the spherically symmetric self-similar solution
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
.

Lemma B.3

(
[
5
,
43
]
)
.

Let
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})=(\overline{Q}(r),\overline{\mathcal{U}}(r)\frac{y}{r})
be the smooth, spherically symmetric self-similar profile obtained in
Lemma
B.2
. Then the following properties hold
:

\displaystyle\overline{Q}>0,

(B.4)

\displaystyle\overline{Q}\geq\widehat{C}^{-1}\langle r\rangle^{-\Lambda+1},\qquad|\nabla^{j}\overline{Q}|+|\nabla^{j}\overline{U}|\leq\widehat{C}(j)\langle r\rangle^{-(\Lambda-1)-j}\,\,\mbox{for any $j\geq 0$},

(B.5)

\displaystyle 1+\partial_{r}\overline{\mathcal{U}}-\alpha|\partial_{r}\overline{Q}|>\tilde{\eta},

(B.6)

\displaystyle 1+\frac{\overline{\mathcal{U}}}{r}-\alpha|\partial_{r}\overline{Q}|>\tilde{\eta},

(B.7)

and, for
r>1
,

r+\overline{\mathcal{U}}-\alpha\overline{Q}>(r-1)\tilde{\eta}.

(B.8)

Here
r=|y|
is the radial variable,
\alpha=\frac{\gamma-1}{2}
,
j
is a natural number, and

\tilde{\eta}>0
is a sufficiently small constant that depends on
(\Lambda,\gamma)
.
Moreover,
\widehat{C}>1
denotes a constant depending on
\Lambda
and
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
, and
\widehat{C}(j)>1
denotes a constant depending on
j
and
\widehat{C}
.

Appendix C
Properties of the Truncated Linear Operator

In this appendix, we collect several properties of the truncated linear operator

\mathcal{L}=(\mathcal{L}_{\zeta},\mathcal{L}_{u})
defined in (
2.20
).
In particular, we describe the decomposition of the phase space
X
into the stable and unstable subspaces,
and summarize the dissipative behavior and regularity features of
\mathcal{L}
that have been used
in our analysis. We first recall a decomposition result for
\mathcal{L}
.

Lemma C.1

(
[
5
]
, Lemma 2.9)
.

Let
\nu_{g}\in(0,1)
, and let
(m,J)
satisfy

m\geq\widetilde{C}\tilde{\eta}^{-1},\qquad J\geq 2m,

for some sufficiently large constant
\widetilde{C}>1
independent of
(m,J,\tilde{\eta})
,
where
\tilde{\eta}
arises from the properties of
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})
in
Lemma
B.3
.
Then

(i)

If
\sigma(\mathcal{L})
is the spectrum of
\mathcal{L}
, then the set

\Sigma=\sigma(\mathcal{L})\cap\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}:\,\Re(\lambda)>-\frac{\nu_{g}}{2}\}

is finite and formed only by the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{L}
,
where
\Re(\lambda)
denotes the real part of
\lambda
.
Moreover, each
\lambda\in\Sigma
has finite algebraic multiplicity.
That is, if
\mu_{\lambda}
is the smallest natural number such that

{\rm ker}(\mathcal{L}-\lambda)^{\mu_{\lambda}}={\rm ker}(\mathcal{L}-\lambda)^{\mu_{\lambda}+1},

then the vector space

V_{\rm{uns}}=\oplus_{\lambda\in\Sigma}{\rm ker}(\mathcal{L}-\lambda)^{\mu_{\lambda}}\,

(C.1)

is finite-dimensional.

(ii)

Denote
\mathcal{L}^{\ast}
as the adjoint of
\mathcal{L}
and

\Sigma^{\ast}=\sigma(\mathcal{L}^{\ast})\cap\big\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}:\,\Re(\lambda)>-\frac{\nu_{g}}{2}\big\},

and define

V_{\rm{sta}}=\big(\oplus_{\lambda\in\Sigma^{\ast}}{\rm ker}(\mathcal{L}^{\ast}-\lambda)^{\mu_{\lambda}^{\ast}}\big)^{\perp},

(C.2)

where
\mu_{\lambda}^{\ast}
is the smallest natural number such that

{\rm ker}(\mathcal{L}^{\ast}-\lambda)^{\mu_{\lambda}^{\ast}}={\rm ker}(\mathcal{L}^{\ast}-\lambda)^{\mu_{\lambda}^{\ast}+1}.

Then both
V_{\rm{uns}}
and
V_{\rm{sta}}
are invariant under
\mathcal{L}
,

\Sigma^{\ast}=\overline{\Sigma}

(
\lambda\in\Sigma
if and only if its complex conjugate

\overline{\lambda}\in\Sigma^{\ast}
)
,
\mu_{\lambda}=\mu_{\overline{\lambda}}^{\ast}
, and the decomposition holds
:

X=V_{\rm{uns}}\oplus V_{\rm{sta}}.

(iii)

The linear transformation
\mathcal{L}|_{V_{\rm{uns}}}:V_{\rm{uns}}\rightarrow V_{\rm{uns}}

obtained by restricting
\mathcal{L}
to the finite-dimensional space
V_{\rm{uns}}
has all its eigenvalues
with real part larger than
-\frac{\nu_{g}}{2}
.
Since
V_{\rm{uns}}
is finite-dimensional, denote

N:=\dim V_{\rm{uns}}.

Then there exists a basis of
V_{\rm{uns}}
such that
\mathcal{L}|_{V_{\rm{uns}}}
is in the Jordan normal form
with
\ell
Jordan blocks, whose sizes satisfy

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\dim J_{i}=N.

More precisely,

\mathcal{L}|_{V_{\rm{uns}}}=\begin{bmatrix}J_{1}&&&\\
&J_{2}&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&J_{\ell}\end{bmatrix}\qquad\,\,\mbox{ with}\quad J_{i}=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{i}&\frac{\nu_{g}}{10}&&\\
&\lambda_{i}&\ddots&\\
&&\ddots&\frac{\nu_{g}}{10}\\
&&&\lambda_{i}\end{bmatrix},

where
\lambda_{i}
,
i=1,2,\cdots,\ell
, are the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{L}|_{V_{\rm{uns}}}
. In that basis,

\Re\big(\bar{w}^{\top}\cdot\mathcal{L}|_{V_{\rm{uns}}}\cdot w\big)\geq-\frac{3\nu_{g}}{5}\|w\|^{2}\qquad\mbox{ for any $w\in\mathbb{C}^{N}$},

(C.3)

where we have used
\Re
to denote the real part and
\bar{w}
is the complex conjugate of
w
.
Moreover, letting
\aleph(t)
be the semigroup generated by
\mathcal{L}
, then

\|\aleph(t)v\|_{X}\leq e^{-t\frac{\nu_{g}}{2}}\|v\|_{X}\qquad\,\mbox{ for any $v\in V_{\rm{sta}}$}.

(C.4)

The next lemma shows that, for sufficiently large parameters
m
and
J
, the unstable subspace constructed above is in fact spanned by smooth functions.

Lemma C.2

(
[
5
]
)
.

Let
\nu_{g}\in(0,1)
.
Then there exist
\tilde{m}
depending on
\tilde{\eta}
, and
\bar{J}
depending on
(C_{0},\nu_{g},\tilde{\eta})

and profile
(\overline{Q},\overline{U})

such that, for all
m\geq\tilde{m}
and all
\bar{J}\geq J_{0}
, the unstable space
V_{\rm{uns}}\subset X
defined in
Lemma
C.1
is formed by smooth functions.

As a direct consequence of Lemmas
C.1
–
C.2
, we obtain the following properties of
\mathcal{L}
.

Lemma C.3

(
[
5
]
, Proposition 1.8)
.

Let
\nu_{g}\in(0,1)
, and let
(m,J)
be fixed as in
Lemmas
C.1
–
C.2
.
Then the Hilbert space
X
can be decomposed as
V_{\text{\rm sta}}\oplus V_{\text{\rm uns}}
,
where both
V_{\rm{sta}}
and
V_{\rm{uns}}
are invariant subspaces of
\mathcal{L}=(\mathcal{L}_{u},\mathcal{L}_{\zeta})
,
and
V_{\rm{uns}}
is finite-dimensional and spanned by smooth functions
\{(\varphi_{i,u},\varphi_{i,\zeta})\}^{N}_{i=1}
.
Moreover, there exists a metric
\Upsilon
of
V_{uns}
such that the decomposition satisfies
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Re\langle\mathcal{L}v,v\rangle_{\Upsilon}&\geq-\frac{3}{5}\nu_{g}\|v\|_{\Upsilon}^{2}\qquad\,\,\,\text{for all}\,\,v\in V_{\rm{uns}},\\
\left\|e^{t\mathcal{L}}v\right\|_{X}&\leq e^{-t\frac{\nu_{g}}{2}}\|v\|_{X}\qquad\,\,\,\text{for all}\,\,v\in V_{\rm{sta}}.\end{split}

(C.5)

Appendix D
Remarks on the Choice of the Initial Data

In this section, we give some remarks on the initial data considered in Theorem
1.1
.
First, for the reformulated system (
2.12
),
we choose the initial data
(Q_{0},U_{0})
in the self-similar coordinates
(\tau,y)
with the following form:

Q_{0}=\widehat{X}(\tau_{0},y)\overline{Q}+\widetilde{Q}_{0}^{*}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\hat{k}_{i}\varphi_{i,\zeta},\quad U_{0}=\widehat{X}(\tau_{0},y)\overline{U}+\widetilde{U}_{0}^{*}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\hat{k}_{i}\varphi_{i,u},

where
\{\varphi_{i,\zeta},\varphi_{i,u}\}_{i=1}^{N}
denotes a normalized basis of
V_{\mathrm{uns}}
, consisting of smooth functions compactly
supported in ball
B(0,3C_{0})
, and the corresponding coefficients
{\hat{k}_{i}}
are fixed in Proposition
6.1
.
Here,
(\widetilde{Q}_{0}^{*},\widetilde{U}_{0}^{*})
is required to satisfy the conditions:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\max\big\{\|\widetilde{Q}_{0}^{*}\|_{L^{\infty}},\ \|\widetilde{U}_{0}^{*}\|_{L^{\infty}}\big\}&\leq\nu_{1},\\
\|\nabla^{K}\widetilde{Q}_{0}^{*}\phi^{\frac{K}{2}}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla^{K}\widetilde{U}_{0}^{*}\phi^{\frac{K}{2}}\|_{L^{2}}&\leq\frac{E}{4},\\
\widetilde{Q}_{0}^{*}+\widehat{X}\overline{Q}&\geq\frac{\nu_{1}}{2},\\
|\nabla(\widetilde{Q}_{0}^{*}+\widehat{X}\overline{Q})|+|\nabla(\widetilde{U}_{0}^{*}+\widehat{X}\overline{U})|&\leq\frac{C}{\langle y\rangle^{\Lambda}},\\
(\chi_{2}\widetilde{Q}_{0}^{*},\chi_{2}\widetilde{U}_{0}^{*})&\in V_{\mathrm{sta}}.\end{split}

(D.1)

On one hand, we consider a Banach space
\mathcal{B}
that incorporates all the norms appearing in
(
D.1
).
There exists a sufficiently small ball
B_{*}\subset\mathcal{B}
such that every element in this ball satisfies the required smallness assumptions shown in (
D.1
).
On the other hand, the restriction

(\chi_{2}\widetilde{Q}_{0}^{*},\,\chi_{2}\widetilde{U}_{0}^{*})\in V_{\mathrm{sta}}

shown in (
D.1
) is equivalent to

P_{\mathrm{uns}}(\widetilde{Q}_{0}^{*},\widetilde{U}_{0}^{*})=0,

which is a finite-dimensional closed restriction. Since projection
P_{\mathrm{uns}}
maps onto the finite-dimensional subspace

V_{\mathrm{uns}}
, there always exists a representative of
(\widetilde{Q}_{0}^{*},\widetilde{U}_{0}^{*})
in the quotient space

\mathcal{B}/V_{\mathrm{uns}}
that satisfies both of the above conditions.
By the form of initial data,
(Q_{0},U_{0})
belongs to an equivalence class in the quotient space
\mathcal{B}/V_{\mathrm{uns}}
. Since
V_{\mathrm{uns}}
is finite-dimensional,
the manifold of initial data that give rise to finite-time implosion has finite codimension.

Second, it follows from (
1.10
),
\tau_{0}=-\frac{\log T}{\Lambda}
, and
y=xT^{-\frac{1}{\Lambda}}=e^{\tau_{0}}x
that

\displaystyle c_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Lambda T^{1-\frac{1}{\Lambda}}}\Big(\mathscr{X}(x)\overline{Q}(\frac{x}{T^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}})+\widetilde{Q}_{0}^{*}(\frac{x}{T^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}})+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\hat{k}_{i}\varphi_{i,\zeta}(\frac{x}{T^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}})\Big),

\displaystyle u_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Lambda T^{1-\frac{1}{\Lambda}}}\Big(\mathscr{X}(x)\overline{U}(\frac{x}{T^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}})+\widetilde{U}_{0}^{*}(\frac{x}{T^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}})+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\hat{k}_{i}\varphi_{i,u}(\frac{x}{T^{\frac{1}{\Lambda}}})\Big).

By the construction of
(Q_{0},U_{0})
, we obtain

c_{0}\geq\frac{\nu_{1}}{2}\frac{1}{\Lambda T^{1-\frac{1}{\Lambda}}}\,\,\Longrightarrow\,\,\rho_{0}\geq\alpha^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\Big(\frac{\nu_{1}}{2}\frac{1}{\Lambda T^{1-\frac{1}{\Lambda}}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}},

and

\|(\rho_{0},u_{0})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C,\qquad\|(\nabla^{K}\rho_{0}\phi^{\frac{K}{2}},\,\nabla^{K}u_{0}\phi^{\frac{K}{2}})\|_{L^{2}}\leq C(E).

Moreover,
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
belongs to an equivalence class in the quotient space
\mathcal{B}/V_{\mathrm{uns}}
.
